§1 — Why: the pane is empty while work runs, and it never ends

The imas-codex coordinator session 6fe78f81 relayed this on 2026-10-12, at the lead's request. At 04:47Z on 98dci4-clu-2002 that session dispatched one review run, r-20261012T044739534705-review-portfolio-4dde24777e04. The host wiring was correct: crew host pid 2116694 started follower pid 2162620, and both write to the pane. The watch stream held the run's baseline row (04:47:43Z) and its dispatched → running row (04:47:56Z). After more than 12 minutes the lead's Monitor pane still read "Status: running · No output available".

The cause is the row policy shipped by a-follower-row-is-worth-a-wake (commits 089cd7741, 79d3a5458, contract 778219ba2). It delivers only a row that asks the coordinator for action, or news nobody caused, and never a start. So a session with work in flight shows nothing. The host also never exits on its own, because Claude Code would not restart it (reckon/crew/session_host.py, module docstring). So the pane stays "running" after the work has ended.

The lead's requirements, in their words: "it should not be overly verbose, but also not empty"; "I should be able to see all work active"; "the monitor should auto stop when there is no live work"; and "the monitor is for me too, I want to see what is going on". The pane has two readers, the coordinator and the lead. The policy that is right for waking the coordinator is not enough for the person watching.

§2 — A run's start is one row on its own session's pane

Deliverable. The row policy in reckon/crew/ticker.py delivers one row when a run that the pane's own session dispatched starts working. The row names the run id, node, lane and model, and time budget. It revises the start-row rule of a-follower-row-is-worth-a-wake for the session's own runs only. A peer session's start, a bare re-announcement, an echo and a replay are still held, so nothing is added to a pane for work that is not its own.

Done when a new test file, tests/test_a_pane_shows_its_own_starts.py, passes:

  • A follower for session S receives exactly one start row for S's run as it moves from dispatched to working, carrying the four fields.
  • A peer session's start delivers nothing.
  • A re-announcement or replay of the same start delivers nothing.

The existing tests that encode the never-a-start rule are updated to the new rule, never deleted, each named in the manifest. The test files that import ticker pass. The negative control, which holds own starts back again, fails the first case. It is logged under the run directory.

Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py, tests/test_a_pane_shows_its_own_starts.py, plus the existing test files whose start-row assertion changes, each named at dispatch.

§3 — Every printed row carries the session's live roll-up

Deliverable. Every row the pane delivers already ends with a counter cell. Ticker._stats in reckon/crew/ticker.py renders it from _fleet_counts in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py, which narrows to a session through _partition_session_rows. That cell gains the session's live nodes, in the ticker's existing abbreviated form, so "all work active" is visible on the latest line without the full stream. No second status block is added to the row, and nothing is computed outside _fleet_counts. It adds no row: a pane with no deliverable change stays quiet.

Done when tests/test_a_pane_row_carries_the_live_rollup.py passes:

  • With three live runs in session S and one in a peer session, the counter cell of S's printed row reports three, names their nodes, and omits the peer's run.
  • When the last live run settles, its row reports none live.
  • The roll-up matches crew(view="live", session=S) for the same fixture.

The test files that import ticker pass. The negative control, which counts every session's runs, fails the first case. It is logged under the run directory.

Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py, reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py (only if _fleet_counts must carry the node names), tests/test_a_pane_row_carries_the_live_rollup.py. This section runs after §2, since both write ticker.py.

§4 — The pane ends when no work is live

Deliverable. When the session has no live run, its follower child prints one idle line, no live work in <session> since HH:MM, and exits. "No live run" is the answer of _population_has_live_work(project, session=...) in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, which already owns that judgement; it is not derived a second time. The idle line extends the final-line path crew follow --lifetime already prints. Today SessionHost.handle drops a request for a project and session already in its children, and tick restarts a keyed child on backoff without removing the key. So when a child settles, the host removes it from its census. The next request for that pair then starts a new child, and backoff never restarts a settled one.

The session host stays alive, and the pane's last line reads idle. When the session next dispatches, the request that dispatch already writes to the host's pipe starts a new follower child for that project and session. The new child replays nothing it has already printed and delivers the new run's start row. No turn is spent re-arming. The host itself still never exits on its own, because a plugin monitor that exits is never restarted in the session (decision pane-end). Claude Code therefore still lists the monitor as running, which this section accepts.

Done when tests/test_a_pane_settles_when_no_work_is_live.py passes over a temporary host state directory:

  • When the last live run of session S settles, the idle line is delivered once and the follower child exits, while the host process stays alive.
  • A follower that observes a peer session (--observe-session) whose run is still live does not settle. A peer session the follower does not observe never holds it open: the population it settles on is its own session plus the sessions it observes, not the whole project.
  • A later request for S starts one new child, which delivers the next run's start row and replays no row the settled child already printed.
  • Two requests for S arriving together start one child.

The existing session-host and follower tests pass. The negative control, which restarts a settled child immediately as today's backoff does, fails the first case. It is logged under the run directory.

Decision: how the pane ends.

  • Host exits. The host writes a closing "no live work" line and exits, so Claude Code shows the monitor as stopped. The next dispatch re-arms the monitor, which costs the session one turn per idle-to-busy transition.
  • Child settles. The follower child ends on a settled summary line, and the host stays and starts a new child when the session next dispatches. No turn is spent, but Claude Code still shows the monitor as running.

Write paths: reckon/crew/session_host.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, tests/test_a_pane_settles_when_no_work_is_live.py.

§5 — The pane speaks seven state words, in pairs

Why. The pane prints whatever word each layer leaves behind: pointer phase, recovery classification, watch state and display alias. On 2026-10-12 it showed both completed_unpromoted and its alias unpromoted for one state. It also printed unpromoted → complete for a run moving forwards, and complete → unpromoted for a reviewed run that went back for fixes. The classifier grew a state for each incident: wait-aged, stopped, unwritten, exited-unfinished and others. Each is right for choosing a recovery action, and each was printed as its own word. Measured over every project's watch stream (11,408 runs, 39,042 transitions), the pane has used 19 state words in 143 distinct pairs. The dwell times show which ones carry no information for a reader:

  • dispatched: median 95 s, followed by working 6,307 times.
  • running: median 49 s, a duplicate of working.
  • promoted: median 3 s, re-announced 365 times.
  • stopped: median 30 s.
  • Awaiting review (median 784 s) and reviewed-awaiting-promotion (median 118 s) follow each other 2,937 times.

Deliverable. The pane uses exactly these seven words (lead's choice, 2026-10-12):

WordMeansInternal states it covers
queuedheld for a lane slotqueued
workinglaunched or runningdispatched, starting, running, working
waitingon a declared external waitwaiting, paused, wait-aged (an overdue wait keeps the word and carries the existing action marker)
haltedstopped short; a decision is neededblocked, needs-help, held, stalled, stopped, abandoned, failed, unreadable, unwritten, exited-unfinished, ended-without-manifest, launch-failed, refused-at-admission
deliveredthe worker finished; not yet landedscoring / completed_unpromoted, promotable / complete
landedpromoted or recordedpromoted, recorded
droppedleft without landingwithdrawn, discarded, departed

Every row is a pair drawn from these words. A run's first row reads new → working or new → queued, where new appears only on the left of a first row. A pair whose two words are the same is never printed. The reason clause, not the state word, names the specific cause: what a halted run asks, what a waiting run waits on, which review a delivered run is in. The internal classifications and their recovery verbs are unchanged. The mapping is one table, owned in one place, which every surface that prints a run's state reads: the row, the run counters and the roll-up of §3. It supersedes DISPLAY in reckon/crew/ticker.py in place, and DISPLAY is retired. The counter bucket labels _WAIT_CELL and _COUNT_FIELD are reconciled with the seven words. So is DISPLAY_HUE, which resolves each word's colour and is keyed today on the old alias words, so every one of the seven words resolves its own hue rather than the dim fallback. Replayed over the same history, this mapping prints 28,408 rows instead of 39,042, in 22 distinct pairs.

Done when tests/test_the_pane_speaks_seven_words.py passes:

  • Every classification and watch state the code can emit maps to exactly one of the seven words. The test enumerates them from the code's own vocabularies, so a new internal state without a mapping fails the test.
  • A rendered row's left and right words both come from the set, and a raw internal word such as completed_unpromoted never appears.
  • A same-word pair prints nothing.
  • A run's first row starts with new.
  • The run counters use the same words.
  • Each of the seven words resolves a hue in DISPLAY_HUE, so no rendered state word falls to the dim fallback.

The existing ticker and follower tests that assert today's words are updated to the new words, never deleted, and each is named in the manifest. The negative control adds an unmapped internal state, and the enumeration test must then fail. It is logged under the run directory.

Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py, tests/test_the_pane_speaks_seven_words.py, plus the existing test files whose expected words change, each named at dispatch. It runs after §2 and before §3, which renders states in its roll-up.

§6 — A pane's remembered states are pane words

Why. §5 maps every state a row draws to one of seven pane words, yet two panes on 2026-10-13 drew completed_unpromoted → delivered after reloading onto that code. A streamed row takes its left word from the state the grid remembers for the run. _seed_ticker_memory in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py fills that memory on attach and reload from the follower's remembered map, which merges the checkpoint with stored history, and both can hold producer states. The memory is copied into the grid unmapped, so a run live across a re-arm or a reload shows its raw state on the left. It recurs at every vocabulary change and at every attach that seeds from stored history.

Deliverable. The grid maps a remembered state to its pane word where it takes the memory in. A remembered state that is already one of the seven words maps to itself; _display_state in reckon/crew/ticker.py blanks delivered, halted, landed and dropped today, so the mapping handles them. No other path writes the grid's memory, so every left word a row draws is new on a run's first row or one of the seven.

Done when a test seeds a grid with a run remembered as completed_unpromoted and another remembered as working, renders a following transition for each, and finds the left words delivered and working. A second test seeds every internal state in PANE_STATES and each of the seven words, and finds each internal state's left word to be its pane word and each seeded pane word's left word to be that same word, delivered, halted, landed and dropped included. The test files importing reckon/crew/ticker.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. The negative control seeds the memory unmapped again, and the first test must then fail. It is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_remembered_state_renders_as_a_pane_word.py.

§7 — A standing line lists your runs that need you

Was §2 of a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view, folded here on 2026-10-13.

Add a row kind to the follower, the needs-you line. It lists every run of the owning session whose state is in the fleet partition's blocked bucket (FLEET_BLOCKED_STATES, the action set less the waiting family) or its unpromoted bucket (FLEET_UNPROMOTED_STATES), both in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py. Each entry gives the node, the state word and the time the run has held that state, as in needs you: 2 unpromoted pta-adopted-read-cost 1h21m, rail-worktree-uv-env 42m · 1 blocked fsa-repair 5m. The line is plain text and reads fully without colour.

An unpromoted run whose review is already stored is marked reviewed in its entry. imas-ambix-f6 lost the only repairable window this way: promotion deletes the pointer and its captured session, so a review read after promotion can no longer be answered by resuming its author.

Emit the line after any transition that changes the set, and again on a cadence whether or not the set is empty. When nothing needs the coordinator the cadence line reads yours: 4 working · nothing needs you. That makes the cadence line a heartbeat, which imas-ambix-f6 asked for, because a quiet pane must not look the same for a healthy follower and a dead one. The cadence defaults to ten minutes and is set with --remind. The list and the line are composed in Python in a new module, reckon/crew/needs_you.py, which reads those two buckets, so a later read view can serve the same list. The follower already derives a needs-you list once, at its end (_needs_you_runs in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py); that row is composed from the new module too, so the pane has one derivation. The follower emits the line from inside the generator _follow_watch_lines in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, in its wait pass where _tick() is already called, because that code can yield to the pane. The on_poll hook cannot carry it: the path map in the measurement plan §4 found that _tick discards on_poll's return value, so nothing it produces reaches the reader.

Done when tests/test_follow_needs_you_line.py runs a follower over a fixture holding two sessions. The owning session has one unpromoted and one working run, and the other session has one blocked run. With the cadence set to one second, the needs-you line appears at least twice in three seconds, names the unpromoted run with its age, and never names the other session's blocked run. A second case with no owned run needing action asserts the cadence line still appears and reads that nothing needs the coordinator. An unpromoted run with a stored review record is marked reviewed. The negative control is executed and logged: with the cadence emission removed, the line appears at most once and the first case fails. The follower's line and a direct call of the derivation in reckon/crew/needs_you.py name the same runs over the fixture. tests/test_every_module_fits_a_worker_window.py passes at head: the follower module stands within about 2,000 estimated tokens of that test's bound, so it gains only the call and the --remind option. The write paths are reckon/crew/needs_you.py (new), reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_follow_needs_you_line.py.

§8 — A drain line says when your own fleet stops working

Was §3 of a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view, folded here on 2026-10-13.

When the owning session's working and queued runs reach zero, emit one drain line. It says either that the session's fleet is drained, or that nothing is working and names how many runs still need the coordinator. Emit it once per change into that state, never on a cadence, because the needs-you line already repeats.

Done when a test moves the owning session's last working run to unpromoted and asserts exactly one drain line naming one run that needs the coordinator. A peer session's working run in the same fixture does not prevent the line. Moving the run on to promoted produces exactly one further drain line saying the fleet is drained. The drain line is the follower's existing settle row (_follower_settle_line in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, which reads no live work in <session> since HH:MM and already fires once per drain, scoped to its own session). The row's line keeps that text as its prefix and gains a tail after it: that the fleet is drained, or how many runs need the coordinator, counted by §7's derivation. The prefix is what tests/test_a_pane_settles_when_no_work_is_live.py matches, so that file is unchanged. tests/test_a_pane_does_not_settle_during_admission.py asserts the whole line by equality (line 340), so its expected text gains the tail for its fixture. The negative control is executed and logged: with the tail withheld, the first case fails because the line names no run that needs the coordinator. tests/test_every_module_fits_a_worker_window.py passes at head. The write paths are reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/crew/needs_you.py, tests/test_follow_drain_line.py and tests/test_a_pane_does_not_settle_during_admission.py. It builds after §7, which creates the module.

§9 — Observing another session is read-only

Was §4 of a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view, folded here on 2026-10-13.

nova-a9 and nova-37 both found that a follower armed with --observe-session ran the recovery sweep over the observed session's runs. In one case it dispatched a review onto a peer's run from the observing session's runtime. The sweep a follower runs must act only on the owning session's runs. _sweep_lapsed_holds, now in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, calls sweep in reckon/crew/resumption.py with the project alone; confirmed on main on 2026-10-16, the call is still sweep(project, dry_run=dry_run). The sweep has two passes. Its resume pass takes every lapsed run from list_live, whatever its session; that is the half still crossing sessions. Its review-reflex pass, dispatch_awaiting_reviews, has scoped itself since 5b98a1c2f (2026-09-23). It resolves an unset session through _sweeping_session (reckon/crew/recovery_repair_dispatch.py), which returns the session whose follower registration this process wrote, and empty, meaning no filter, for a process holding none. tests/test_review_lane_follows_its_run.py::test_the_sweep_bookends_only_its_own_sessions_runs holds that. The review the incident reports came from the reflex before it was scoped.

Deliverable. sweep takes the session it sweeps as an explicit argument and passes it to both passes. The resume pass keeps only that session's runs, and the reflex pass is called with session= set rather than left to _sweeping_session. The follower passes its owning session. One mechanism then scopes both passes for any caller, including one that holds no follower registration, which is what the watcher in §37 will be. A sweep called with no session, the hand-run sweep from the command line or the MCP surface, behaves as today. The path map in the measurement plan §4 is stale: its reckon/cli.py line numbers predate the split of that file.

A run whose session has no follower then has no sweeper. The watcher that sweeps such a session, under the decision locked below, is §37, built on this section's explicit session.

Done when tests/test_follow_sweep_is_session_scoped.py runs a sweep through a follower for one session over a fixture where both that session and an observed session each own one run whose hold has lapsed. Only the owning session's run is resumed, asserted through a stub launcher that records its calls. A second fixture gives each session one run in the scoring state with a composed review brief. A sweep called with one session's id, from a process holding no follower registration, dispatches the review for that session's run only. That case exercises the explicit session, not _sweeping_session. The test files that import reckon/crew/resumption.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Two negative controls are executed and logged. With the resume pass ignoring the session, both lapsed runs are resumed and the test fails. With the reflex pass called without session= from the unregistered process, both scoring runs are reviewed and the test fails. The write paths are reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/crew/resumption.py and that test.

§10 — A run nobody will come back to is reported, never capped

Was §5 of a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view, folded here on 2026-10-13.

The worker cap retired in a-dispatch-returns-without-its-worker §8 guarded against a real hazard in the wrong form: runs nobody returns to. The census imas-ambix-61 took at 13:17 UTC on 2026-09-23 found finished, unpromoted pointers whose owning coordinator was no longer live. Among them were imas-codex ship-s14-20260917 at 52 hours, ship-s10-20260918, and two runs of reckon ship-crpc-20260921. A session may keep its runs as long as it needs them. The report exists so a run is never forgotten, and it limits nothing.

A pointer is forgotten when two things hold. Its classification is terminal and unpromoted: complete, blocked, failed, promotable, scoring or abandoned. And its owning session has had no live follower registration, and no dispatch, for at least a threshold that defaults to six hours. The list is derived in Python in a new module, reckon/crew/forgotten_runs.py. It joins the classifier with the follower-liveness and unattended-age readers that already exist in reckon/crew/runs.py (_follower_liveness, _unattended_seconds), which today drive only the watcher's sweep. It must not reuse overdue_unreconciled_runs in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py: that list feeds the dispatch fence, which refuses, and this one only reports. The threshold is a parameter of the derivation. The list is served as a crew read view, crew(view="forgotten") and reckon crew view forgotten --project P at the default threshold, rather than as a new verb, because the crew surface serves reads through crew view. One row per pointer names the run, node, session, classification, age and the recover action. A pointer whose session is unknown counts as forgotten once it passes the threshold, because absence is not ownership.

Done when tests/test_forgotten_runs_are_reported.py builds a temporary RECKON_HOME holding three pointers. One is complete and owned by a session that has a live follower. One is complete and owned by a session with neither follower nor dispatch for seven hours. One is running and owned by that same session. The view lists exactly the second, with its age and recover action. The derivation called with an eight-hour threshold lists nothing. The negative control is executed and logged: with the follower-liveness condition removed, the first pointer is listed too and the test fails. No dispatch path consults the list: the test reads every module under reckon/crew/ whose name begins dispatch and finds none naming the new function or module, and as its positive control it reads reckon/crew/forgotten_runs.py with the same instrument and finds the function's name there, so the absence is a reading rather than a failed search. tests/test_crew_view_serves_every_view.py, tests/test_mcp_vocabulary_is_complete.py and tests/test_ledger.py, which walk every read view, add no failure against base. The write paths are reckon/crew/forgotten_runs.py (new), reckon/mcp_views.py for the view's branch and name, and that test.

§11 — Push the reader's own obligations

Was §6 of a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view, folded here on 2026-10-13.

§2 and §3 share reckon/cli.py and run in order. §4 needs the sweep decision locked.

Done when the follower surfaces the reader's own obligations unprompted (§2 and §3 in order; §4 needs the sweep decision).

Moved from followup f-afkywiv-001 on a-follower-keeps-your-work-in-view by work-is-a-section-not-a-followup §4; evidence against main at 1135b00d9 (line numbers may have moved since): plan active impl 0.0

§12 — The critical-path coverage cell, and why it waits

Was §10 of monitor-renders-what-it-is-given, folded here on 2026-10-13.

The counter block answers how much work is in flight and says nothing about which work. A fleet can read 7w all day while the plan on the critical path has nobody on it — measured on nova, 2026-09-18: the roadmap ranked the critical path first for a whole sprint while twelve runs closed plans already between 85 and 99 percent complete, and the path itself carried zero workers. A coverage cell on the row is what makes that visible where a coordinator already looks, rather than in a report nobody opens mid-wave.

This section owns the cell and not the figure, and the split is the project's rule rather than a preference here. The coverage figure is a derived fact a reader without a pane would also want, so it is computed once in Python and consumed by the surface. Deriving it a second time inside the renderer would be two implementations of one truth — the same defect with a longer fuse, because nothing fails when the copies drift.

The figure belongs to work-scheduling-surface §3 — roadmap coverage per open path, computed from live pointers whose process is alive rather than from transition events. That section is queued behind its own plan's push-status node because the two share reckon/roadmap.py. Its coordinator offered to let this cell read live pointers directly and to have §3 adopt the same helper afterwards. That offer is declined: it places the first implementation of a derived fact inside a renderer and then asks the owning module to follow it. Waiting costs a queue position and buys one derivation.

Done when the row carries a coverage cell for the critical path whose value comes from the roadmap's own derivation rather than from any figure computed in ticker.py, asserted against a fixture in which coverage and the working count disagree — they must differ, or the assertion passes on a renderer showing the wrong number.

§13 — The session host removes its FIFO when it ends

Was §9 of a-follower-lives-as-long-as-its-session, folded here on 2026-10-13.

Deliverable. When reckon crew host stops, because its owner ended or it was signalled, it removes the FIFO it was handed. The entry point removes the FIFO only when its owner ends before the first request; once it has become reckon crew host, nothing does, so every session that dispatched leaves one stale FIFO in the runtime directory. The entry point's own removal does not hold either. It runs only when the wait loop sees the owner gone, and the entry point traps no signal, so a monitor that Claude Code stops at session end exits before reaching it. Measured 2026-10-07: two sessions that never dispatched (Claude pids 2484216 and 2489693) ended and left their FIFOs behind with no entry point alive. So the entry point also removes its FIFO on TERM, HUP and INT. The host derives the path from its owner pair and the same runtime-directory order the entry point uses.

Done when a test in tests/test_session_host.py starts the host on a FIFO named for a fake owner, ends the owner, and finds the FIFO gone; a test in tests/test_session_host_plugin.py sends TERM to a waiting entry point and finds its FIFO gone; and the session-host census reports stale_fifos=0 after one session that dispatched and one that did not have both ended. Each test fails on the current code, which is its negative control: the host test because nothing removes the FIFO once the entry point has become reckon crew host, and the entry-point test because no trap removes it on TERM.

§14 — A run that changes state across a producer reload still wakes its session

Was §16 of a-follower-lives-as-long-as-its-session, folded here on 2026-10-13.

A run that finishes while the producer reloads never wakes its session. Measured on 2026-10-11: the lane plan review r-20261011T002225507072 was observed working by a transition row at 00:22:59Z. The producer reloaded at 00:28:22Z, after peers landed reckon code, and the run's completion was first published by the replacement as a baseline row with a null from_state. The session's follower and producer were live throughout (follower since 2026-10-10T18:03Z, producer pid since 23:41Z), but a baseline row is inventory, not news, so nothing woke the coordinator. It sat idle for 52 hours on a review that had finished in six minutes.

The replacement producer has its predecessor's last published state for every run in the event stream it appends to. A run whose state differs from that last published state is a transition, and the replacement publishes it as one, with the predecessor's state as from_state. Only a run the stream has never named is a baseline.

Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py (watch_follow, which emits the baseline and transition rows) and a new test module.

Done when a test writes an event stream whose last row for a run is working, marks the run complete, starts watch_follow as a replacement producer over that stream, and asserts the run's first new row is a transition from working to the completed state. A run absent from the stream still arrives as a baseline. On main the completed run arrives as a baseline with a null from_state, so the test fails there.

§15 — A held request is retried without leaving the queue

Why. The pane flickers. The queue runner, admit_once in reckon/crew/queue_admission.py, retries each held request by deleting its queued pointer in _launch_queued and running a full dispatch, which the lane refuses and queues again. Every pass therefore emits a withdrawal, a dispatch and a queueing for a request whose state never changed, and the pane draws queued → dropped and dropped → queued each time. Measured on 2026-10-13: 445 of the project's 725 transitions in two hours, 61%, were this cycle, and one held plan review cycled 112 times.

Deliverable. Before it releases a queued pointer, admit_once asks the lane the two questions dispatch would ask, the lane gate (_dispatch_lane_gate in reckon/crew/dispatch.py) and the session's lane allowance. A request the lane would still hold is left exactly as it is: the same run id, phase queued, no event. Only a request the lane will admit is released and launched, as today.

A request that cannot launch is not retried every pass. The request that cycled 112 times was never waiting on the lane: its dispatch failed every pass with a CrewError because its brief file no longer exists, and each pass released it, failed and restored it. A request whose last attempt failed for a reason other than a lane hold keeps its queued pointer and its recorded failure and is not attempted again until something it depends on changes, such as its brief, its plan or its lane configuration, or until a bounded back-off elapses. Its first such failure is reported once to its session.

Done when a test runs admit_once over a queued request while the lane holds it, and finds the pointer unchanged and no transition appended to the project's event stream; with the lane open, the same request launches. A second case queues a request whose brief file is missing and runs admit_once three times: the first pass records the failure, and the next two leave the pointer unchanged and append nothing to the stream. The test files importing reckon/crew/queue_admission.py show no added failure against base. The negative control releases the pointer before the admission check again, and the held case must then find a withdrawal in the stream. It is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/queue_admission.py and tests/test_a_held_request_stays_queued.py.

§16 — The pane draws no round trip

Why. Even with §15, a run can leave a state and come back within seconds: a requeue, a lane change, a resume that is refused. The pane draws both halves, so a reader sees two rows that cancel each other.

Deliverable. RowPolicy in reckon/crew/ticker.py holds a row that takes a run away from the word the pane last showed for it, and drops it together with the return when the run comes back to that word within the settle window. A departure that does not come back within the window is delivered as it is today, so a real withdrawal still reaches the pane.

Done when a test feeds queued → withdrawn → dispatched → queued within the settle window for a run the pane shows as queued, and finds no row; the same departure with no return delivers its dropped row once the window passes. The test files importing reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. The negative control removes the round-trip rule, and the first case must then show rows. It is logged under the run directory. This section runs after §6 and after the-suite-is-green-at-head §15, which also write ticker.py. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_the_pane_draws_no_round_trip.py.

§17 — A standing lift is not announced again on every restart

Why. On its first pass after it starts, reckon/crew/lift_watch.py emits a baseline row for every lift in force, and the pane draws each one as new → working, "lift in force". A producer restarts on every reload, so a standing lift is announced again each time. A peer pane measured it on 2026-10-13: the same codex-sub lift announced at 09:32:06, 09:35:57, 09:36:15, 09:37:26, 09:37:51 and 09:38:03 with no change between them. A baseline row is inventory of what already exists, not something that happened.

Deliverable. A pane draws a lift's grant and its end, and draws a standing lift once when the pane first sees it. A baseline row for a lift the pane already showed, or for one a reloaded watcher re-announces, draws nothing. The mechanism is the row policy in reckon/crew/ticker.py: RowPolicy remembers, per pane, the lift groups it has drawn as in force, and holds a baseline lift row for a group already drawn. A lift row carries no run id (run_id is None and the group is in node, built in reckon/crew/lift_watch.py), so the run-keyed _reported memory cannot hold it as it stands. The memory is keyed on the lift's group, a grant records it, and an end forgets it, so the next grant is drawn again. Carrying the announced lifts across a restart in the lift watcher would instead emit no baseline at all, so a pane that attaches afterwards would have nothing to draw, and the first-attach case below would fail.

Done when a test restarts the lift watcher with one lift standing and finds no new pane row, a new grant and an end each still draw one row, and a pane attached for the first time still shows the standing lift once. The test files importing reckon/crew/lift_watch.py or reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. The negative control re-announces the baseline on restart, and the restart case must then show a row. It is logged under the run directory. Three nodes write ticker.py (this section, §16 and the-suite-is-green-at-head §15), so they run one at a time. This section goes first, because the re-announced lift is the row a reader sees after every producer reload. §16 runs after it. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py, reckon/crew/lift_watch.py (only if the test needs a seam there) and tests/test_a_standing_lift_is_announced_once.py.

§18 — A reload waits for a clean, importable tree

Why. Between about 19:30 and 19:37:27 UTC on 2026-10-13, a session edited reckon/crew/ticker.py and reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py in place in the main checkout. Every project's producer imports that checkout and reloads when its code stamp moves, so producers reloaded onto half-edited code. imas-ambix's producer failed with NameError: _fleet_live_nodes at about 19:34:52. nova's failed at 19:37:18 inside format_watch_transition with AttributeError: 'Ticker' object has no attribute '_node_cell'. Did you mean: '_job_cell'?. That was a rename half-applied: the file still compiled and imported, and only failed when a row was rendered. The RabbitPy, imas-codex, nova and imas-efit producers all restarted between 19:37:04 and 19:38:06. The follower's reloader already defers onto code that does not import (_replacement_imports in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, pinned by tests/test_follower_reload_is_safe.py). That check passed this tree, because a mid-edit rename can import cleanly and still fail at its first use.

Deliverable. A producer or follower whose code stamp has moved reloads only onto a tree it can trust. While any tracked file under reckon/ differs from HEAD, the reload is deferred, and the same applies when any module the process and its children load fails to import, not only the entry point. A deferred reload keeps the running image, says so once in the dim deferred line, and retries when the code changes again. A clean, importable tree reloads as today.

Done when tests find three things. First, a tracked reckon/ file left modified against HEAD defers the reload, and the process keeps delivering on its current image. Second, a module that raises NameError at import defers the reload. Third, committing the edit, so the tree is clean against HEAD, lets the reload proceed. The existing syntax-error deferral still passes. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, remove the dirty-tree check; the modified-file case must then reload onto the half-edited tree. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths are reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_reload_waits_for_a_clean_tree.py.

§19 — A producer that crashes is restarted

Why. When nova's producer exited at 19:37:18 UTC (see §18), nothing started another. Lingering cannot be enabled on the compute node, so --ensure had armed the producer as a plain background process rather than the Restart=always unit. The seat stayed empty until a coordinator, prompted by a peer, ran crew watch --ensure 34 s later (pid 2952488). Every follower of a project without a producer stops hearing its runs, and a run that finishes in that gap is the known case where a reload hides a run's completion.

Deliverable. A producer that crashes while its project still has an armed follower is replaced without a coordinator. On the fallback route, _start_watch_producer in reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py runs the producer under the inline supervisor _WATCH_PRODUCER_SUPERVISOR, which today waits once and exits with the producer's status. It gains a bounded restart with back-off. The discriminator is the producer's exit status. The supervisor restarts a crash. That is an error exit, a status from 1 to 127; an exception that escapes the watch loop propagates and exits 1. It is also a death by a signal the producer does not trap, which wait() reports as a negative status (for example SIGKILL, or a fault such as SIGSEGV). A deliberate stop is left alone. A lease that expired, or an empty project, exits zero. The signals the producer traps, SIGTERM and SIGHUP, exit 128 + signum through its own handler (SystemExit(128 + signum) in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, pinned by tests/test_producer_says_why_it_stops.py). The supervisor also forwards a signal it receives itself to the producer and does not restart after it. The Restart=always unit route in reckon/crew/watch_unit.py already restarts and is left as it is.

Done when a test arms a fallback producer, makes it raise inside the watch loop, and finds a new producer holding the seat within the back-off. A producer killed by SIGKILL is restarted too. A producer stopped by expiring its lease (exit zero), or by SIGTERM (exit 143), is not restarted, and neither is a producer whose supervisor was itself signalled. A producer that keeps crashing stops being restarted after the bound, and says so. The test files importing reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, remove the restart; the seat must then stay empty after the crash. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths are reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py and tests/test_a_crashed_producer_is_restarted.py.

§20 — One fixed-column row

Why. The lead asked for a clear ticker in a fixed column order. Each row shows time, stage, job, transition and duration, then the four counters, the lane's rate in tokens per second, the model and the effort. It carries no messages, because a pane has no room for them. Before this change, review rows repeated the reviewed job's name with a review-of- prefix and a twelve-hex digest, and retries carried random suffixes. A request the admission pass launched under a new run id drew as queued → dropped followed by a fresh new → working.

What landed. reckon/crew/ticker.py draws one fixed-column row: time, stage, job, transition, elapsed, the w h d q counters, rate, model and effort. Trailing cells are dropped when the pane is narrow. The job cell drops the review prefix and digest, and numbers a run's retries 1, 2 and so on (_job_name, _job_cell). Dispatch writes admitted_from on a run the admission pass launches from a queued request. The fleet fold in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py (fleet_transitions) carries the request's history onto that run instead of drawing a drop. Each row's counters are the ones the producer held at that transition.

How it landed. The coordinator wrote the source change (1eb387165) in a scratch worktree rather than dispatching it to a worker node, which this plan's build contract does not allow. That came after the in-place edits to the main checkout that §18 records. Four local-lane nodes brought the tests to the new row: r-20261013T200709695919-ticker-row-producer, r-20261013T200755442568-ticker-row-layout-tests, r-20261013T202033377549-ticker-row-render-tests and r-20261013T202359933004-ticker-row-lane-tests. An independent review of the source (r-20261013T213053886992) found nothing blocking. The change merged as 1fc67c671. Removing free text also removed two things the pane still needs. One is the marker for a wait whose probe never ran (§22). The other is the clause that told a standing lift from a new grant, which is why §21's repeated lift looks like news.

§21 — A standing lift survives the follower's own reload

Why. §17 stops a standing lift being drawn again when the producer restarts. It is still redrawn when the follower restarts. A peer pane measured it on 2026-10-14: lift codex-sub new → working at 03:00:44, 04:16:51, 04:20:05, 04:26:11, 05:43:48 and 06:02:53 (+02:00), with no change to the lift between them. Five of the six rows came 10 to 40 seconds after a merge of reckon code into main, at 03:00:34, 04:19:27, 04:25:58, 05:43:33 and 06:02:37. A merge moves the code stamp, so the producer and the follower both reload. The producer's lift sweep starts again with no memory and writes a baseline lift-in-force row, as §17 intends. §17 holds that row back using the drawn lift groups in RowPolicy._lift_groups in reckon/crew/ticker.py, but that set lives only in the follower process. An in-place reload restores the pane's run states, through the checkpoint it hands across (path.reseed) and the stored history (follow_checkpoint.seed_states, which skips rows that carry no run id). Nothing restores the lift groups, so the new image draws the baseline as a new lift.

Deliverable. A pane's memory of the lift groups it has drawn survives the follower's own in-place reload and its re-arm. The memory travels in the reader checkpoint an in-place reload hands across, and in the stored history a re-arm reads. It is restored into the row policy before the first row is fed. §17's other cases are unchanged: a pane attaching for the first time draws a standing lift once, and a grant and an end each draw one row.

Done when a test drives the follower's in-place reload with one lift standing and already drawn, feeds the producer's re-arm baseline lift row, and finds no pane row. A re-arm that restores from stored history behaves the same way. A pane attaching for the first time still draws the standing lift once, and a grant and an end after the reload each draw one row. The test files importing reckon/crew/ticker.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, leave the lift groups out of the carried memory; the reload case must then draw new → working. The control is logged under the run directory. Base the node on c30a0c628 or later, which carries §18's reload rules. Three sections of work write ticker.py, and they run in this order: the-suite-is-green-at-head §15, then this section, then §16, then §22. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/crew/follow_checkpoint.py (only if the re-arm case needs it) and tests/test_a_standing_lift_survives_a_follower_reload.py.

§22 — A row marks a wait no probe has run for

Why. A run can declare a wait that no probe has ever checked, for example because its probe never launched or cannot read the condition. Its row must not look like a wait whose condition is still being tested. The superseded monitor-renders-what-it-is-given plan drew a marker for this in the row's reason clause (_unprobed_wait). §20 removed free text from the row, and the marker went with it. 1eb387165 removed _unprobed_wait and UNPROBED_MARKER, and e81059843 removed test_an_unprobed_wait_renders_differently_from_a_pending_one. Such a run now reads as ordinary waiting. The facts are still on the event. declares_a_wait remains in reckon/crew/ticker.py, but 1eb387165 also removed probe_has_run. Whether a probe has run is now read from the event's wait_condition_state field, which that helper used to read.

Deliverable. A row whose event declares a wait no probe has run for draws ? directly after its destination state word, in the state cell's spare column. STATE_WORD is 10 and the longest pane word, delivered, is 9, so marked and unmarked rows align and the row does not widen. The en dash is not used, because the row already uses it for an absent value.

Done when a test renders two rows for a waiting run, one declaring a wait no probe has run for and one whose probe has reported. The first shows waiting? and the second waiting. Both rows are the same width, and every later cell starts in the same column. A row that declares no wait carries no marker. The test files importing reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, remove the marker; the first case must then fail. The control is logged under the run directory. This section runs after §16. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_row_marks_an_unprobed_wait.py.

§23 — A lift is drawn once per pane, however many projects it follows

Why. On 2026-10-14 a workstation-wide uncapped codex-sub lift, codex-sub-1792002586-64d825, was granted at 18:29:46Z. Every project's producer writes lift rows into its own event stream, and nine streams carried this grant, stamped between 18:29:46Z and 18:30:10Z. The session host runs one crew follow per project, and all of them write to the session's one pane. Each follower keeps its own memory of the lift groups it has drawn (§17, §21). So a power_convertor session's pane that followed three projects drew lift codex-sub new → working three times: at 20:29:47 twice and at 20:30:10 (+02:00), each row carrying that project's counters. The lead read it as noise, and it is: one grant is one event.

Deliverable. A lift row is drawn once per session pane for each lift id and transition, by whichever of the session's followers reaches it first. The session's followers share one claim per (session, lift id, transition). Today the row has no structured lift id. node carries the lift's group, and the id appears only inside the free-text detail, which §20 removed from the rendered row. So _lift_row in reckon/crew/lift_watch.py adds the lift's id to the row as a lift_id field, set on the row that _watch_transition returns. That leaves recovery_watch.py untouched, and the claim keys on that field, never on text parsed from detail. A claim is one file, created with an exclusive create (O_CREAT|O_EXCL) in a per-session directory under the crew home's watch directory. A shared JSON record would not do: followers start within milliseconds of each other on the same grant, and a read-modify-write lets two of them win. The session host may run on another node, so the claim must work on the shared home, and an exclusive create does. A follower takes the claim when it prints the row, after the observer hold and §16's departure hold have decided. A follower that claimed first and then held the row would leave no pane drawing it. The claims are not kept in follow_checkpoint's per-follower record, because that record is per grid, and a per-grid memory is the defect. They do not grow without bound: a claim is pruned once its lift has ended and the end row has been drawn, and any claim past a fixed age is pruned. Only lift rows change. Run rows belong to one project and are drawn as before. §17's group keying and §21's carried memory still hold within one project. Another session's pane draws the same lift once, on its own claim.

Done when a test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, runs three followers for one session over three project streams. Each stream carries the same lift-granted row, built by lift_watch's own row builder and carrying lift_id, at a different observed time. Across the three followers' output there is exactly one lift row. The lift's end, carried in all three streams, also draws once. A second lift id draws its own row. A follower for a different session draws the grant once on its own pane. The real crew home is untouched. The test files that import reckon/crew/ticker.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, drop the claim: the three-project case must then draw three rows. The control is logged under the run directory. This section runs after §22 and does not touch reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py, which §14 writes. Write paths: reckon/crew/lift_watch.py, reckon/crew/ticker.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_lift_is_drawn_once_per_pane.py.

§24 — Scratch housekeeping never prints to a pane

Why. The lead saw removing worker scratch directory … (0 bytes) and removed worker scratch directory … on a session pane. They come from bare print() calls: in remove_worker_scratch in reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py, which runs at every promotion and discard through promotion_release, and in garbage_collect_scratch in reckon/crew/gc.py. When a promotion runs inside a follower or producer, for example a delivered review promoted automatically, that process's stdout is the pane. The removal is already recorded: the release result carries scratch_removed_paths with each path and size, and scratch_warning for an over-budget tree, and crew complete and crew gc emit those results. The print adds nothing a reader needs and puts housekeeping on the pane.

Deliverable. remove_worker_scratch and garbage_collect_scratch write nothing to stdout. Every removal stays named, with its path and size, in the result they return, and so in what crew complete and crew gc emit. A dry-run crew gc still names each directory it would remove, in its report.

Done when a test promotes a run whose scratch directory exists, in-process with stdout captured, and finds nothing about scratch on stdout. The result names the removed path and its size. A second case runs crew gc --scratch --apply through the CLI and finds the removed path in the emitted JSON. A third finds a dry run's would-remove directory in the emitted JSON. The negative control, restoring the print in remove_worker_scratch, turns the first case red. The tests that pinned the printed lines, tests/test_a_runs_scratch_ends_with_the_run.py and tests/test_worker_scratch_lives_with_its_run.py, assert on the result instead. The test files importing either module show no added failure against base.

Write paths: reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py, reckon/crew/gc.py, tests/test_a_runs_scratch_ends_with_the_run.py, tests/test_worker_scratch_lives_with_its_run.py and tests/test_scratch_housekeeping_is_not_printed.py.

§25 — A producer reload republishes no run that has not moved

Why. Every merge into main reloads the producer. Since §14, the replacement seeds its first sweep from the stream's last published state for each live run. It publishes a run that moved as a transition, and a run that has not moved as a fresh baseline with a null from_state. Those baselines are drawn. A peer session's run, delivered at 21:37:15Z, has three such rows in the stream after its delivery, at 21:44:01Z, 23:10:20Z and 05:27:07Z, one per producer reload with no change between them. The lead saw it drawn again as new → delivered. Each reload repeats this for every live run that has not moved. The stream already carries each of those runs' state, so the row is neither news nor needed inventory. A follower attaching cold builds its inventory from the live pointers through runs.watch_stream_cursor, not from the stream's baseline rows.

Deliverable. In _publish_watch_transitions in reckon/crew/runs.py, the first sweep appends no row for a live run whose state equals the state the stream last published for it. A run that moved is still a transition, and a run the stream never named is still a baseline.

Done when a test writes a stream whose last row for one run is complete and for another is working. It keeps the first run complete and moves the second to complete, runs a replacement producer's first sweep, and finds no new row for the first run, a transition for the second, and a baseline for a third run the stream never named. The negative control, appending a baseline for an unchanged run as before, turns the first case red. The manifest lists every reader of the project's .events stream under reckon/ and how it folds rows. No reader may rebuild a run's state only from rows written since the producer started; if one does, the section stops and reports it. The §14 test file and the test files importing reckon/crew/runs.py's stream functions show no added failure against base.

Write paths: reckon/crew/runs.py, tests/test_a_replacement_producer_publishes_transitions.py and tests/test_a_reload_republishes_no_unmoved_run.py.

§26 — A pane does not settle while queued work is being admitted

Why. On 2026-10-14 this session's pane printed no live work in f653840f… since 23:08, then no live work … since 23:17 (+02:00). A review the session had queued at 21:12:35Z was admitted at 21:17:37Z as r-20261014T211737909806 and ran to completion, and the pane drew none of it. The session's follower was gone: crew(view="live") read session_attached: false, and the producer listed no follower for the session. The cause is the admission handover. _launch_queued in reckon/crew/queue_admission.py deletes the queued request's pointer and then calls dispatch(), which builds the worktree before it writes the admitted run's pointer. For those seconds the session has no unfinished run. The follower's settle check (_population_has_live_work in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, held for FOLLOWER_SETTLE_INTERVAL_SECONDS, 1 s) reads the empty population, prints the idle line and exits with the settled status. Nothing brought the pane back. dispatch() does ask the session's host for a follower on every CLI dispatch, the admit service's included (reckon/crew/dispatch.py). But _session_host_fifo in reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py finds the host from the caller's own CLAUDE_PID. The admit service, pid 3923354 on the same node, is a background service with no Claude process of its own, so its ask finds no FIFO and falls back without a follower. Keeping the existing follower through the handover is therefore the repair. A re-arm from the admit service would need the session's host recorded on the queued request, and that is not this section's work. The second idle line was not only repeated: it went out at the moment the session's work began.

Deliverable. A follower settles only after its population has held no unfinished run for a settle grace longer than an admission handover. The grace is one named constant, two minutes, a bound on the dispatch the admit service makes, and it replaces the one-second hold. A run that appears inside the grace, an admitted run above all, keeps the follower and is drawn as usual. A population that stays empty for the whole grace settles once, and its idle line names the moment the last unfinished run was seen, as it does today. A queued request still counts as unfinished work, so a follower never settles while its session has a request queued. The fix lives on the follower's side; the admission order is not changed here.

Done when a test drives the follower's settle decision with an injected clock and sleeper through the handover. The session's queued pointer is present; it is removed and no pointer exists for less than the grace; the admitted run's pointer appears. The test finds no settle row emitted and the admitted run's start drawn. A population that stays empty past the grace settles once, with the idle line naming the last-seen time. A population holding a queued pointer never settles. The test files that import reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. For the negative control, restore the one-second hold: the handover case must then settle before the admitted run appears. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_pane_does_not_settle_during_admission.py.

§27 — A resumed run is not halted on its previous attempt's process

Why. On 2026-10-15 a power_convertor pane drew converter-units-simscape as working → halted at 08:58:17 (+02:00) while its worker was alive on a repair round, and iter-vessel-evolution as delivered → halted → working at 08:42. The stream rows give the order. At 06:58:11Z the run's fifth attempt delivered. At 06:58:17Z the repair round's resume advanced attempt.json to attempt 6 (attempt_started_at 06:58:17Z) and cleared the delivered manifest, as a-run-has-one-live-writer §4 now does before a resumed worker spawns. The resume removes the exit record, so the exit plays no part; the pointer and the stream do. The pointer still named attempt 5's process, which had exited, so liveness read False. The producer's death branch in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py (alive is False on a working row) then read the newest stream. That was still attempt 5's, ending in a result record, so the row became ended-without-manifest. Seconds later attempt 6's stream existed holding only its system start record, and the same branch wrote interrupted. Both are drawn as halted. At 06:58:30Z the new worker registered and the run read working. Across every project stream between 15:30Z on 14 October and 07:00Z on 15 October there were 32 such halts, each followed by working within 120 s: 13 in power_convertor, 6 in imas-efit, 5 in RabbitPy, 3 in imas-codex, 3 in reckon and 2 in nova. Each one tells a reader to resume work that is already resuming.

Deliverable. A death is read only of the current attempt's process. Before the producer's death branch applies, it checks that a worker record naming the attempt attempt.json names exists. The resume path (_prepare_attempt_records in reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py) removes worker.json and exit.json together before attempt N's supervisor starts. So in the window, attempt.json names attempt N, there is no worker record at all, and the pointer still names an earlier attempt's process. That process's absence says nothing about attempt N. Such a run reads as starting, which the pane draws as working, within STRANDED_LAUNCH_BOUND_SECONDS (900 s, in reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py) of attempt N's attempt_started_at, the bound a launch with no worker record is already given. The deferral carries that bound itself. _stranded_launch skips any pointer that names a pid, and this pointer always names the earlier attempt's, so it cannot catch this case. Past the bound the deferral reports the run as a stranded launch. A resume whose launch has already recorded a failure reads as that failure at once. Once attempt N's worker record exists, the death branch applies to its process exactly as today. A run with no attempt.json is classified as it is today.

Done when a test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, builds a run whose attempt.json names attempt 6 started a moment ago, with no worker.json or exit.json (as _prepare_attempt_records leaves it), whose pointer still names attempt 5's exited process, whose manifest is the cleared template, and whose newest stream is attempt 5's, ending in a result record. It finds the run classified starting, not ended-without-manifest. With attempt 6's stream present and holding only a system record, it still reads starting, not interrupted. With attempt 6's worker record written and its process alive, the run reads working. With attempt 6's process then dead, the death branch reads it as today. With attempt 6 past STRANDED_LAUNCH_BOUND_SECONDS and no worker record, it reads as a stranded launch. A recorded launch failure reads as that failure at once. The test files that import the changed modules show no added failure against base. For the negative control, apply the death branch without the attempt check: the first case must then read ended-without-manifest. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py, reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py and tests/test_a_resume_is_not_halted_on_its_last_attempt.py.

§28 — A resumed attempt's stall clock starts at the attempt

Why. §27 stopped a resume's first seconds from reading as a death when the producer can see the old process has gone. A producer on another host cannot see it: liveness reads unknown, and the run reaches the stall check instead. On 2026-10-15 at 09:37:16Z, a repair round started attempt 3 of r-20261015T075223938148-jev-routes-with-the-session-admission-and-the-lift. At 09:37:17Z the producer, on clu-2018 with the worker on clu-2002, wrote stalled: "liveness unknown, quiet 17m". Four seconds later the new attempt's stream moved and the run read working. The 17 minutes were the previous attempt's. Quiet time is already capped by the attempt clock: _run_stream_quiet_seconds in reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py takes the lesser of the stream's silence and the time since the attempt started. But _attempt_started_seconds reads attempt_started_at from the pointer first, and falls back to attempt.json only when the pointer has no such field. In the resume window the pointer still carries the previous attempt's start, while attempt.json already names the new one. So both terms read 17 minutes. Across all project streams between 09:00Z on 14 October and 09:40Z on 15 October there were 4 such stalled rows, each followed by working within 120 s: 3 in reckon, 1 in power_convertor.

Deliverable. _attempt_started_seconds reads both the pointer's attempt_started_at and attempt.json's, and returns the later of the two that parse. The attempt record is written when a resume begins, before the pointer is rewritten, so the later start is the current attempt's. A resumed attempt is therefore never stalled on the quiet its predecessor left; it stalls only once it has itself been quiet for the stall window. A run carrying only one of the two is measured from that one, as today.

Done when a test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, builds a run whose pointer liveness reads unknown, whose pointer still carries the previous attempt's attempt_started_at from 17 minutes ago, whose newest stream was last written 17 minutes ago, and whose attempt.json names an attempt started a second ago. It finds the run not stalled. With that attempt started longer ago than the stall window and the stream still quiet, the run reads stalled as today. A run with no attempt record and a stream quiet past the window reads stalled as today. The test files that import the changed module show no added failure against base. For the negative control, restore the pointer-first read of attempt_started_at: the first case must then read stalled. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py and tests/test_a_resumed_attempt_is_not_stalled_on_its_predecessor.py.

§29 — A halt the run has already left is not drawn

Why. On 2026-10-15 the nova run r-20261015T091903690374-fast-read sat blocked, waiting for a ruling, for 40 minutes before its coordinator noticed. The producer wrote every transition: dispatched → working at 09:19:30Z; working → stopped at 09:19:38Z, the coordinator's crew stop; stopped → working at 09:19:50Z, its crew redispatch --backend codex; and working → blocked at 10:19:16Z. Replaying those rows through PaneRowPath and one Ticker, as the follower composes them, reproduces the pane exactly. RowPolicy in reckon/crew/ticker.py holds a coordinator row for SETTLE_WINDOW (20 s), so that a later coordinator row of the same run can supersede it. A row that is not a coordinator row supersedes nothing; that rule keeps the terminal echo complete → recorded from swallowing a completion. The redispatch's stopped → working arrived 12 s into the stop's window. Holding the stop had already marked the run told, but the policy's memory of the run's state still read working, because a held row is not remembered until it is released. So the return read as a re-derivation of the state the pane already showed, and was dropped as silent before the recovery exception was consulted, and the settle hold was left standing. The held stop was released after it, and the pane drew working → halted for a run that was already working on its new backend. It showed the run halted for the next hour. §30 covers why the block that ended that hour then printed nothing.

Deliverable. A held settle row is never released after a later row has moved its run on to a live pane word: working, waiting or queued. If the later row returns the run to the word the pane last showed for it, the held row and the return are dropped together, as §16 drops a round trip. A redispatch or a resume inside the window is such a return, so the pane goes on showing the run working. Otherwise the held row prints at once, ahead of the later row, and the later row is judged with the run told, as if the held row had already printed. A later row into a settled state, such as the terminal echo, still supersedes nothing. A return outside the window prints as it does today: working → halted, then halted → working.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_halt_the_run_left_is_not_drawn.py, drives PaneRowPath for the run's own session with an injected clock, and renders each released row, in order, through one Ticker. Fed the fast-read rows, it releases exactly two rows: the start, and the block drawn working → halted. The rows are dispatched → working; working → stopped classified stopped 8 s later; stopped → working 12 s after that; a flush past the settle window; working → blocked classified blocked an hour later; and a flush past its 300 s noise window. With the return 40 s after the stop instead, it releases the start, working → halted, halted → working and the block, as today. With stopped → waiting 12 s after the stop, it releases working → halted and then halted → waiting as the second row arrives. A held working → complete followed by complete → recorded inside the window still releases the completion. The test files that import reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed files against base. For the negative control, let a later row that is not a coordinator row supersede nothing again: the first case must then release a row whose destination is stopped. The control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_halt_the_run_left_is_not_drawn.py.

§30 — A released run row is never blank

Why. Ticker.render in reckon/crew/ticker.py keeps its own memory of the word it last drew for each run. It returns an empty string when a row's left and right words match. RowPolicy decides whether a row is news from its own memory of raw states, so it releases rows that the renderer then draws as nothing, and the follower records each of them as delivered. After §29's stale halt, the fast-read block was released as a coordinator row when its 300 s noise window passed. It was drawn halted → halted and printed nothing. The follower histories under ~/.config/reckon/crew/watch, read at 2026-10-15T11:09Z across six projects, hold 550 released rows with empty text out of 6,101. Nineteen of them were duties the coordinator never saw: 13 blocked, 2 wait-aged, and one each of stalled, unreadable, stopped and exited-unfinished. Most of the rest asked for nothing: 194 promotion echoes (completed_unpromoted → complete, drawn delivered → delivered), 264 queue cycles drawn queued → queued, and 43 lift rows.

Deliverable. One predicate decides whether a row is news when its destination pane word equals the word the pane shows for its run. RowPolicy and Ticker.render both read it. Such a row is news when it carries a duty: its recovery classification is in ACTIONABLE_RECOVERY_CLASSIFICATIONS, or it declares a wait (declares_a_wait). The policy withholds every other same-word row and remembers its state, so a promotion echo or a queue cycle is no longer recorded as delivered. The renderer draws a same-word row that carries a duty. It takes the left word from the row's own from_state where that differs, and otherwise repeats the destination word, so halted → halted prints instead of vanishing. Every run row the row path releases therefore renders to a non-empty line. A direct render of a same-word row that carries no duty still returns an empty string, as the existing renderer tests expect. Lift rows are keyed on their group and decided by _feed_lift, so they are outside this section.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_released_run_row_is_never_blank.py, drives PaneRowPath with an injected clock, and renders each released row, in order, through one Ticker, as the follower does. First case: the pane has drawn working → halted from working → unreadable, and an unreadable → blocked classified blocked arrives a minute later. That row renders non-empty. For a run the pane has drawn working → delivered, completed_unpromoted → complete classified promotable is not released. For a run the pane shows as waiting, a baseline waiting row that declares a wait renders non-empty. The last case covers every ordered pair of distinct run states drawn from the members of PANE_STATES, except the lift states. A path that has released dispatched → first is fed first → second, and each step is flushed past every hold. Every row it releases renders non-empty. The test files that import reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. Any test whose assertion this changes is named in the manifest and updated, never deleted. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed files against base. For the negative control, make the renderer return an empty string for every same-word row again: the first case must then render blank. The control is logged under the run directory. This section runs after §29, which also writes ticker.py. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_released_run_row_is_never_blank.py.

§31 — A duration override from the environment is read one way

Why. §26 added RECKON_FOLLOWER_SETTLE_GRACE, and its review noted that it parses a duration override in its own way. Duration overrides for tests and operators are each parsed by hand. The parsers read the same kind of value but disagree on what they accept:

  • _terminal_manifest_grace_seconds and _stop_grace_seconds in reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py accept zero and above.
  • producer_lease_seconds in reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py accepts only values above zero.
  • _settle_grace_default in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and stop_derivation_budget in reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py accept any float, a negative one included.

A negative settle grace or derivation budget is therefore taken as given, while the same text for the stop grace falls back to its default. Every new override restates the parse, which is how the four rules came apart.

Deliverable. One function reads a duration override. It takes the variable name, the default, a lower bound and whether that bound is itself accepted. An unset, blank or unparseable value, or one outside the bound, gives the default; it never raises. Each site above calls it with the bound stated here. Both grace sites in dispatch_launch.py and the settle grace take zero, inclusive: a zero grace ends at once or settles at once, and it is a valid choice. The derivation budget takes zero, exclusive: a zero budget would skip the derivation it exists to bound, so zero gives its default. A lease takes zero exclusive, because producer_lease_seconds refuses zero today (a lease of zero would end every producer on its first wake-up), and so does the same rule in reckon/crew/runs.py, which is migrated too. Any other site in reckon/ that parses a duration from the environment by hand is migrated only where its callers want a duration back for an unset value. A site whose callers read "unset" as distinct from a value stays as it is, and the census names it with that reason. One such site is _positive_seconds in reckon/mcp_deadlines.py: its None walks a three-level fallback chain. The manifest lists every migrated site. The function lives in a module that every caller can import without a cycle. Migrating a site can change what it accepts, and each such change is stated rather than hidden. The census already counts _cross_node_fast_s, _cross_node_slow_s, _cross_node_read_window_s and _served_signature_ttl in reckon/serve.py, which parse with no guard at all, so an unparseable value raises there today. The manifest lists every migrated site with its accepted range before and after. Where a value that used to be accepted now gives the default, the manifest names the value and the site's reason for refusing it. No default changes.

Done when a test pins the function for an unset, a blank, an unparseable, a below-minimum and a valid value, with the environment set through the test's own monkeypatch. A test for each migrated site shows that a negative value gives that site's default. A zero value gives the producer lease its default and is accepted as zero by the stop grace. The manifest carries a census of every function in reckon/ that turns an environment value into a duration, whether it calls float directly or through a helper such as _positive_seconds. It lists each by file and name, migrated or left with a stated reason. A grep for float( next to an environment read finds only the new function. The test files that import the changed modules give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new tests. Negative control: with the function's minimum check removed, the below-minimum case fails; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py, reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, the module that holds the function, any other migrated module the manifest names, and tests/test_a_duration_override_is_read_one_way.py.

§32 — A delivered row does not round-trip on its worker's own manifest rewrite

Why. At 14:55:19Z on 2026-10-15 the pane drew r-20261015T143441103048-a-lane-family-has-one-owner as delivered → working, and at 14:55:20Z as working → delivered. The run never left its first attempt. Its worker had written a complete manifest at 14:54:24Z, and it rewrote that manifest at 14:55:19Z during its final turn. The producer, on another node with the worker's liveness unknown, read the rewrite once as running, then as completed_unpromoted one second later. The project stream holds three such terminal → working → terminal flips within 5 s since 2026-09-25. §16 holds a row that takes a run away from the word the pane last showed and drops it with the return inside the settle window. This pair was drawn anyway, after today's §29 and §30 changes to the settle rule.

Deliverable. First, reproduce the pair through PaneRowPath and one Ticker at head, as §29 did: the run shown as delivered, then the departure and its return one second apart. Record in the manifest which rule drew them: §16's hold not applying to a departure from delivered, §30's release drawing the return, or something else. Then repair it in RowPolicy in reckon/crew/ticker.py, so that a departure from delivered that returns to delivered inside the settle window draws nothing, as §16 already does for queued. A departure from delivered that does not return within the window still draws once the window passes. §29's and §30's tests stay green.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_delivered_row_does_not_round_trip.py, drives PaneRowPath with an injected clock and renders each released row through one Ticker. Fed delivered → working and working → delivered one second apart for a run the pane shows as delivered, it releases no row. With no return, it releases the departure once the window passes. The test files that import reckon/crew/ticker.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with the repair removed, the round trip releases both rows; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_delivered_row_does_not_round_trip.py.

§33 — A delivered run owes no review before the reflex has had its turn

Why. On 2026-10-15 the stop hook blocked this coordinator's turn twice on a review-missing duty that the review reflex was about to discharge itself.

  • Run r-20261015T162611840986-a-run-s-overrides-have-one-reader wrote its complete manifest at 17:11:21Z. Its worker exited at 17:12:45Z, and the reflex launched its review at 17:14:18Z. The hook raised review-missing about 22 s after the manifest write and blocked the stop at 2m59s. A hand dispatch issued from the hook's command found the reflex's review already working.
  • Run r-20261015T165405320126-the-follower-lease-uses-the-store-writer wrote its manifest at 17:16:36Z. The hook blocked the stop at 46 s, while the worker was still in its final turn. The coordinator dispatched the review by hand at 17:16:54Z.

The duty is derived from the run's classification (scoring) in reckon/crew/obligations.py, which reads true as soon as the manifest is complete. The reflex, in contrast, acts only on an exited run, and only on its next sweep. So between the manifest write and the reflex's first attempt, measured today at about three to seven minutes, every coordinator is told to dispatch a review the reflex is about to dispatch, and the two race.
