The claim-first check keeps the race from making a duplicate, but the hook still holds the turn on a wait the coordinator cannot shorten. That is the case REVIEW_QUEUED_DUTY_KIND already exempts for a refused attempt.

Where the fault is. A worker that is provably alive with a complete manifest already raises no duty. deferred_outcome in reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py holds it at running, and no duty kind maps from running. The duty fired because both workers ran on clu-2002 while the producer that derives obligations ran on clu-2018. There, a worker's liveness reads unknown, and deferred_outcome tests alive is True directly rather than the stream-aware verdict that a-seat-held-elsewhere-is-not-restarted §10 gave process_gone. So a worker on another node, still in its final turn with a complete manifest, reads scoring at once, and scoring maps to review-missing.

\n

Deliverable. One change, in reading a run that has delivered.

  • deferred_outcome treats a worker as still running when the hoisted verdict says it is not gone and no exit record exists for the run's current attempt, not only when alive is True. The exit record (exit.json, written by the run's supervisor on the shared home) crosses nodes. Its presence ends the run at once, so an exited worker on another node reads scoring as soon as its exit is recorded. It does not wait for its stream to go quiet past the 900 s stall window, which would delay its review by a quarter of an hour. A worker on another node with unknown liveness and a moving stream then reads running while it finishes, exactly as a provably-live worker on the same host does, and raises no duty. A worker whose liveness is False, or unknown with a still stream, reads as today.

No post-exit window is added. After a worker exits, the reflex launches its review on its next sweep, which was about 90 s after the exit on 2026-10-15. The duty may read review-missing for that interval, and a hand dispatch issued from it is refused as a duplicate by the claim-first check, so it costs nothing. The sweep cadence is the follower's own --sweep-interval and is recorded nowhere the obligations deriver reads, so a window keyed on it would have no source.

Done when a test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, classifies a run launched on another host. The run has a complete manifest, its pointer liveness is unknown, and its stream is moving inside the stall window. The test finds it reads running, and that obligations derive no review duty for it. With the stream past the window it reads scoring, as today. The same cross-host run with an exit record for its current attempt, and a stream still inside the window, reads scoring at once. The stop hook holds no turn for the first case. The test files that import reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py or reckon/crew/obligations.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with deferred_outcome testing alive is True again, the first case reads scoring; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py, reckon/crew/obligations.py and tests/test_a_delivered_run_waits_for_the_reflex.py.

§34 — A resumed run is not read by its previous attempt's status

Why. On 2026-10-15 run r-20261015T183541384225-a-claimless-review-is-refused-at-store stopped as blocked, with a manifest written at 19:31:06Z. The coordinator read the blocker, widened the run's scope and resumed it, and attempt 2 started at 19:44:46Z and was working. The stop hook still listed the run as [blocked], telling the coordinator to "resolve the blocker before resuming the run", which had already happened. The coordinator had to acknowledge the duty by hand to end its turn. _reset_delivered_manifest_status in reckon/crew/resumption.py clears only the supervisor's done words (complete, failed) before a resume, and deliberately leaves blocked, waiting and in-progress as they are. So the classifier reads attempt 1's blocked as the state of attempt 2 until the new worker's first manifest write.

Deliverable. A manifest status written before the run's current attempt started is not that attempt's status. When the manifest file's modification time is earlier than the attempt's start, read through _attempt_started_seconds in reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py, the classifier reads the run from its current attempt's liveness, not from the old status word. A live or moving new attempt reads as working. The old word governs again once the new worker rewrites the manifest. The rule covers every non-terminal word the reset leaves, not blocked alone, and the reset itself is unchanged.

Done when a test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, classifies a run whose manifest reads blocked with a modification time before its current attempt's start, and whose current attempt is alive. It reads working, and obligations derive no blocked duty for it. The same run, with the manifest rewritten after the attempt started and still reading blocked, reads blocked. A run with no recorded attempt start reads as today. The test files that import reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with the attempt-start comparison removed, the first case reads blocked; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py and tests/test_a_resumed_run_is_not_read_by_its_old_status.py.

§35 — A blocked run with a recoverable session still needs a waiver to promote

Why. tests/test_crew_promotion.py::test_a_session_only_in_the_stream_also_refuses and ::test_complete_command_accepts_a_reasoned_resume_path_waiver fail on main. The base of every-review §18 (050a6556c, 2026-10-15 22:47) already failed them, and they failed identically at base and head in §18 and §19. Each builds a CLI run whose manifest reads blocked, with a stream and no pid or exit record. Each expects crew complete --gate not-run to refuse with the resume-path message, because promotion deletes the pointer a resume needs. The run now promotes instead (DID NOT RAISE CrewError, and exit 0 where 1 was expected). _require_resume_waiver in reckon/crew/promotion_checks.py returns early unless the classification is blocked. §33, merged as e22d78980 seven minutes before that base, made deferred_outcome hold a delivered run at running while the stream-aware verdict says the worker is not gone and no exit record exists. A fixture run with a fresh stream and no exit record reads exactly so. §33's gate covered only the test files that import recovery_classification.py or obligations.py directly, and test_crew_promotion.py reaches them only through promotion. Confirming this cause is part of the node.

Deliverable. A run whose fresh manifest reads blocked and whose session is still recoverable refuses promotion without --waive-resume-path, whatever the delivered-run hold reads. §33's behaviour is kept: a delivered run on another node, still finishing its final turn, reads running and raises no review duty.

Done when the two tests pass at head unchanged, and tests/test_a_delivered_run_waits_for_the_reflex.py passes unchanged. The manifest records the classification the first test's fixture run reads at e22d78980's first parent and at head. The test files that import reckon/crew/promotion_checks.py or reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from those two tests now passing. Negative control: with the fix reverted, the two tests fail again; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/promotion_checks.py and reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py. Build it after every-review §19 lands, because §19 also writes promotion_checks.py.

§36 — A resume's first seconds are not drawn halted

Why. On 2026-10-16 the repair reflex resumed run r-20261016T013153737687-the-roadmap-lists-come-out-in-one-order. Its attempt.json recorded attempt 2 starting at 02:09:29Z. The monitor drew the run delivered → halted, in red, at 02:09:29, and halted → working at 02:09:32. The new worker's record (worker.json) appeared at 02:09:38, and its stream (resume-1.jsonl) at 02:09:51. So a run being resumed was shown dead for the seconds between its attempt starting and its new process being visible. It is intermittent: the resumes of §35 at 02:39 and of every-review §20 at 03:43 went delivered → working directly, so the false row appears only when a producer poll lands inside that window. Inside the resume window, recovery_classification drops the previous attempt's manifest and sets liveness to unknown (§34). It then reads the attempt "from its own clock, exactly as a run on another host is". For a run on another host, that clock is the stream-aware process_gone verdict. Which branch draws it halted is unknown, and the obvious candidate is ruled out. process_gone asks _newest_stream_in_motion (reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py), which asks _newest_stream_shows_work (reckon/crew/recovery_wait.py). The previous attempt's stream holds assistant records (140 in this run), and _run_stream_quiet_seconds caps stream quiet at the new attempt's own clock of about one second. So that chain reads the stream as in motion, not gone. The halted reading came from somewhere else. Candidates include a producer poll that read the pointer before attempt.json was visible, and an arm that does not pass through process_gone. The node reproduces the reading first and names the branch from what it observes.

Deliverable. Inside the resume window, an attempt whose own start is within STRANDED_LAUNCH_BOUND_SECONDS (900 s, in reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py), and which has neither a stream nor a process record of its own yet, reads as starting or working, never halted. The start is resolved by _attempt_started_seconds, never by the pointer's raw attempt_started_at, which inside this window can still hold the previous attempt's time. The previous attempt's stream does not decide its liveness. Once that bound has passed with no stream or process record from the new attempt, the run reads as today, through the existing stranded-launch rule (_resumed_launch_is_stranded), so a resume that never launches is still reported. No second window constant is added. §34's rule, that the previous attempt's manifest is not this attempt's report, is kept.

Done when the node first reproduces the reading at base. A test, with the crew home pointed at a temporary directory, builds a run shaped like the observed one. The previous attempt delivered a complete manifest, and its stream.jsonl ends with assistant and result records, as a finished worker's does. A new attempt is recorded in attempt.json one second earlier, with no worker record and no stream of its own. At base the run reads halted, and the manifest names the branch and the property of the fixture that produced it. At head the same run does not read halted, and obligations raise no duty for it. The same run with its attempt start past STRANDED_LAUNCH_BOUND_SECONDS, and still no stream or worker record, reads as today. §34's test file and tests/test_a_delivered_run_waits_for_the_reflex.py pass unchanged. The test files that import reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py or reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with the branch the node named restored to its base behaviour, the first case reads halted again; the control is logged under the run directory. If the halted reading cannot be reproduced at base from this fixture, the node stops and reports what the fixture read rather than changing code. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_classification.py, reckon/crew/recovery_liveness.py and tests/test_a_resume_is_not_drawn_halted.py.

§37 — The watcher sweeps a session nobody is following

Why. Once §9 confines a follower's sweep to its own session, a run whose session has no live follower has no sweeper: its lapsed hold is never resumed, and its finished run is never reviewed by the reflex. The decision who-sweeps-an-unfollowed-session is locked on the measurement in the measurement plan §7. The project watcher sweeps a session once it has had no live follower for ten minutes. It records itself as the writer of each action, and it leaves alone a session whose pointers name another host. Present coordinators re-arm within a median of 12 seconds and 90 percent within 9.7 minutes, so ten minutes separates a re-arm from an absence. When this section was filed, no such sweep existed on main. The fleet's producer is reckon crew watch in its default following mode, which runs watch_follow and then watch_ticker in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py (crew_watch in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py). watch in reckon/crew/runs.py is reached only with --once or --exit-on-empty. The sweep belongs in both loops, and the following loop is the one the fleet runs. This section first named only runs.watch, and the 2026-10-16 build wired the sweep there alone. Its worker reported the gap, and the coordinator confirmed it by tracing the crew watch branch.

Deliverable. On its tick, the watcher finds each session with live runs in the project that has had no live follower for ten minutes and whose pointers name this host. It calls sweep with that session passed explicitly, as §9 makes possible, and with itself as writer. The watcher holds no follower registration, so it never relies on _sweeping_session. Without the explicit session the reflex would read no filter and review every session's runs. A session with a live follower, or one unattended for less than ten minutes, is not swept by the watcher.

Done when tests/test_the_watcher_sweeps_unfollowed_sessions.py runs the watcher's tick over a fixture holding one session for each case below. A session unattended for eleven minutes is swept: its lapsed run is resumed and records the watcher as writer, and its scoring run's review is dispatched. A session unattended for nine minutes is not swept. It owns a scoring run with a composed review brief, which the reflex would offer if it read no session filter. A session whose pointer names another host is not swept. A session unattended by pointer age for eleven minutes but whose follower registration is live is not swept, with the registration's lock held as reckon/crew/follower_registration.py judges liveness. This case guards the liveness half of the rule against a sweep keyed on age alone, which would double-resume a followed session. In the same tick, a scoring run belonging to the nine-minute session is not reviewed, so the reflex under the watcher is confined to the swept session. The test files that import reckon/crew/runs.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with the watcher calling sweep without the session, the nine-minute session's scoring run is reviewed and the test fails; the control is logged under the run directory. The test drives both loops: watch_ticker's tick sweeps the eleven-minute session as watch's does, and a second control, with the sweep removed from watch_ticker only, leaves the following loop's eleven-minute session unswept and fails. Write paths: reckon/crew/runs.py, reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py and tests/test_the_watcher_sweeps_unfollowed_sessions.py. Build it after §9 has landed.

§38 — A run resumed out of a stop is drawn working again

Why. On 2026-10-16 run r-20261016T050200605849-the-audit-shares-the-plan-parse stopped on a blocker, and the pane drew it working → halted at 06:28:04Z. The coordinator resumed it, and the producer recorded blocked → working at 06:30:42Z. The pane drew nothing until halted → delivered at 06:38:58Z. For eight minutes it showed a red halted row for a run whose worker was working. Another run in the same pane, pace-path…-by-its-report, showed the same halted → delivered with no working row between. In reckon/crew/ticker.py, a row from the pane's own session prints when it is a coordinator row or a start. START_KINDS holds only ("dispatched", "working") and ("starting", "working"), on the assumption that a resumed attempt passes through starting. The producer instead records the resume as blocked → working. That kind is an observer row outside COORDINATOR_KINDS, so it is dropped, and the pane keeps the stop word.

Deliverable. A transition from a stop state into working, for a run of the pane's own session, is a start. Stop states include blocked, exited-unfinished (hyphenated, as the classifier emits it and as PANE_STATES keys it), stalled and abandoned, and any other state the pane draws as halted. The pane draws the run working again, as it draws a fresh launch. §32's hold is unchanged. A run the pane drew as delivered or landed that a producer re-reads as working is a manifest rewritten mid-turn, not resumed work, so it stays held. A peer session's resume prints nothing, as a peer's start prints nothing.

Done when a test drives the ticker for a pane owning one session through the observed stream's rows. These are working → blocked, then blocked → blocked, then blocked → working, then working → completed_unpromoted, with the rows more than the settle window apart. The blocked → blocked row is the one the producer wrote at 06:28:09Z and 06:29:38Z. Without it, the stop is still held as a noise opener when the resume arrives. The run's remembered word is then still working, and the resume is withheld by the same-word check before the start check is reached, at base and at head alike. The test finds the resume withheld at base, and at head the pane prints a working row between the halted row and the delivered row. The same sequence for a peer session's run prints no working row. A run the pane drew as delivered and then read as working is still held, as §32 requires. The test files that import reckon/crew/ticker.py give the same pass and fail ids at head as at base, apart from the new file. Negative control: with the stop-to-working kinds removed from the start set again, the working row is not printed and the test fails; the control is logged under the run directory. Write paths: reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_a_resume_is_drawn_working_again.py.

§39 — The sweep test runs as the production watcher does

Why. §37's last review scored 102 with one follow-on. tests/test_the_watcher_sweeps_unfollowed_sessions.py adds a finished run, r-late-review, for the re-arming session. Neither the head pass nor any of the four controls offers it. The cause is a fixture artefact. The test process holds a follower registration for the attended session, so when the session is dropped from the watcher's sweep call, _sweeping_session (reckon/crew/recovery_repair_dispatch.py, read by reckon/crew/recovery_review_acceptance.py) resolves that registration, and the reflex reviews the attended session's run only. The production watcher holds no registration, as §37 states. For it, _sweeping_session returns the empty string, which the reflex reads as no filter, so a dropped session would offer every session's composed review. So §37's session-confinement control fails, but through a path the production watcher never takes.

Deliverable. The test drives the watcher's tick from a process that holds no follower registration, as the production watcher does. Any registrations the fixture needs to mark a session as followed are held by a separate helper process, which takes each registration's advisory lock the way a live follower does (reckon/crew/follower_registration.py judges liveness by the held lock). That keeps the attended session live while the process running the tick holds no registration of its own. With the session passed, the reflex offers only the swept session's run. With it dropped, the reflex reads no filter and also offers the other sessions' composed runs, r-late-review and r-attended-review among them. The comments describe that. No change under reckon/.

Done when the test asserts that the tick's process holds no follower registration, and that the reflex offers only the swept session's scoring run. The test file passes. Negative control: with the session dropped from the watcher's sweep call, the reflex offers the other sessions' composed runs as well, and the test fails, logged under the run directory with the offered run ids. Write paths: tests/test_the_watcher_sweeps_unfollowed_sessions.py.

§40 — A reload probe that times out is retried, not read as a broken image

Why. On 2026-10-17, after a-worker-cannot-write-your-home §12 merged as 773e7e542 (03:01:57Z), the follower reported "reckon crew follow deferred its reload: the new image does not import (TimeoutExpired …)". The image did import. checkout_trust.reckon_imports catches subprocess.SubprocessError, which includes TimeoutExpired, and returns it as a failed import. In _FollowerReloader.poll in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, a failed import sets self.deferred_stamp = current_stamp, and the next poll returns early while the stamp is unchanged. Its echo says so: "retrying when the code changes". So one slow probe keeps the old image running until the next merge, however long that is. That night the next merges came within minutes. On a quiet evening, the producer and every follower would serve old code for hours, and nothing would report it. The probe is usually well inside its bound. The plan review measured 6.8 to 11.1 s (median about 9 s) against the 30 s bound, at load 29 on 98dci4-clu-2002. One timing at 03:10Z took 30.9 s. Timeouts are therefore intermittent load spikes, not a steady state, and making the probe faster is not the repair. The next paragraph is the first draft's account, kept as it was written. The obligations snapshot it cites went stale at 02:57:48Z, before the merge, so the deferral did not cause it.

First draft's account. The producer on 98dci4-clu-2018 reloaded only at 03:11:06Z, after repeated deferrals. Meanwhile every session's obligations snapshot went stale: this session's was last written at 02:57:48Z and read 839 s old at 03:11Z. Timed on the fleet node 98dci4-clu-2002 at 03:10Z, the reload probe _FOLLOWER_RELOAD_PROBE in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py took 30.9 s, against FOLLOWER_RELOAD_PROBE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 30.0 in reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py. It compiles every module under reckon/, then imports every one in a fresh interpreter. The package has grown to the bound. So a loadable checkout reads as one that "does not import" whenever the node is busy. Every reload a merge triggers now risks a stall of the snapshots every coordinator's hooks read. a-worker-cannot-write-your-home §11 makes any change to the package trigger a reload, so this happens on every merge.

Deliverable. checkout_trust.reckon_imports tells a probe that ran out of time apart from a probe that ran and failed. On a timeout, the follower records and echoes "the import probe timed out after N s", leaves deferred_stamp unset, and probes again after a retry interval. The interval starts at 60 s, doubles after each further timeout up to 15 minutes, and resets when the stamp moves or a probe answers. The freshness window alone is not enough. runs.FOLLOWER_FRESHNESS_SECONDS is 1 s, so it would rerun a 30 s probe almost back-to-back on a node that is already slow. The run review of the first delivery found this. The deferral is echoed once per contiguous run of timeouts, and its recorded reason does not repeat the timeout clause. An image that really fails to import is still deferred until the code changes, and the deferral still names the module. The probe's text and its bound stay as they are. The other callers of reckon_imports keep their meaning: a timeout is still not a proof that the image loads.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_reload_probe_timeout_is_not_a_broken_image.py, drives _FollowerReloader.poll with a moved code stamp, a clean tree, and a probe whose subprocess raises TimeoutExpired. The deferral line names a timeout, not "does not import". A poll inside the retry interval does not probe, and a second timeout does not echo a second line. With the probe then succeeding and the stamp unchanged, the first poll past the interval probes again and reloads. A second case, a checkout with a module that fails to import, is deferred and names the module. The poll after it, with the stamp unchanged, does not probe again. Negative control: with the reloader again setting deferred_stamp on a timeout, the retry case fails on the missing second probe. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. The coordinator runs the importer population at merge. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/checkout_trust.py, reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py and the test file.

§41 — A reload runs the committed head, not the shared working tree

Why. Every follower and producer imports reckon from the shared main checkout, through _import_root in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. That same checkout is where every coordinator merges, and where some sessions edit by hand. _FollowerReloader.poll defers a reload whenever checkout_trust.tracked_reckon_tree_is_dirty finds a tracked file under reckon/ differing from HEAD. That guard is right about what not to run, because a tree mid-edit can import and still fail at first use. But it makes every session's follower and producer wait on whichever session last left the tree dirty. The lead's monitor over two days showed a run of such deferrals:

  • M reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py, M reckon/crew/watch_unit.py
  • M reckon/crew/dispatch_picker.py, …
  • MM reckon/crew/picker/lane_context.py, MM reckon/crew/picker/prompts.py, MM reckon/crew/promotion.py
  • M reckon/crew/dispatch.py
  • M reckon/crew/promotion.py, M reckon/crew/promotion_checks.py, M reckon/crew/rollout.py
  • M reckon/crew/dispatch_admission.py, a merge on 2026-10-17 that lost an index.lock race and held the file staged for about three minutes.

While the producer defers, every session's obligations snapshot reads "not current". The lead reported this as preventing live work by other sessions. A merge in progress, a conflict being resolved, or one session's hand edit is enough.

Deliverable. A reload runs the commit at HEAD, never the working tree.

  • When the code stamp moves, the reloader materialises HEAD's tree into a directory keyed by its commit, under the crew's cache. It probes that directory with the existing import proof, and re-executes with that directory as the import root.
  • A dirty shared checkout no longer defers a reload. Its uncommitted edits are not what the reloaded image runs.
  • A failed import still defers, naming the module, and §40's timeout retry applies unchanged.
  • Materialised trees beyond the newest few are pruned, and the pruning prints what it removed.
  • The code stamp a follower compares is the shared repository's HEAD commit, read with git rev-parse from the shared checkout. The checkout's path is carried across the re-execution. The stamp is never a hash of the files the image runs, which after a reload are a frozen copy and would never change again. A follower and producer started from the shared checkout move onto a materialised tree at their first reload.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_reload_runs_the_committed_head.py, builds a temporary repository with a reckon package and commits it. It moves HEAD to a second commit and leaves a tracked file under reckon/ modified in the working tree. Then:

  • _FollowerReloader.poll reloads without deferring, and the import root it re-executes with holds the second commit's tree. The working tree's uncommitted edit is absent from it.
  • A HEAD whose module fails to import still defers, naming the module.
  • After more commits than the retention count, the oldest materialised trees are removed, and the removal is printed.
  • A reloader running from a materialised tree sees a commit made afterwards in the shared repository, and reloads onto it.
  • The stale-producer warning, which compares a producer's recorded stamp with the current one, agrees with the commit stamp. A producer on the current HEAD is not reported stale.

Negative control: against the base tree, the dirty-tree case defers with "tracked files are modified against HEAD". The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. The coordinator runs the importer population at merge. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/checkout_trust.py and the test file. They were widened during the build to reckon/crew/follower_registration.py, reckon/crew/runs.py, reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py and tests/test_stale_producer_warning_waits_for_its_reload.py, where the producer's recorded stamp and the stale-producer readers live.

§42 — A reviewed head lands without touching the shared index

Why. Coordinators land a reviewed worker head with git merge --no-ff in the shared checkout, ~/Code/reckon. Promotions write their own commits there at the same time. Several merges have lost the .git/index.lock race after writing the merged files and before committing:

  • this session's merge of a-worker-cannot-write-your-home §14 on 2026-10-17;
  • two of reckon-b9's merges on 2026-10-16;
  • reckon-8a's merge aborted on an autostash.

Each left staged files until someone repaired it by hand. While the tree was dirty, every session's follower and producer deferred its reload (§41). The §14 recovery showed a safer path. Build the merge with git merge-tree --write-tree and git commit-tree, which touch neither the index nor the working tree, then move main to it. The lead asked for that path to become the default way a coordinator lands work.

Deliverable. A verb, reckon crew merge --project <p> --head <sha> --message <subject> --body <text>, that lands a reviewed head on the checked-out primary branch, in this order:

  1. Pin the base, B = HEAD.
  2. Build T = merge-tree --write-tree B head. A conflict refuses, naming the conflicted paths, with nothing written.
  3. Build C = commit-tree T -p B -p head with the given subject and body. Refuse a message carrying an AI-attribution trailer or no body.
  4. Land it with git merge --ff-only C, which either completes or leaves the checkout untouched. If index.lock is held, retry briefly. If HEAD moved, rebuild from the new HEAD, a bounded number of times.
  5. Push C and confirm the remote head equals it.

It prints one JSON document naming B, the head, C as read back by rev-parse, and the remote head. An optional --candidate <sha> lands a merge commit already built, whose parents must be the pinned HEAD and the head. That lands exactly the tree a population measured. Unrelated tracked edits elsewhere in the checkout, such as run records under docs/state, do not block it, as they do not block a fast-forward. The reckon-build skill's integrate step, in skills/reckon-build/SKILL.md, names the verb as the way to land a reviewed head.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_reviewed_head_lands_without_touching_the_index.py, builds a temporary repository with a bare origin, a reviewed head on a side commit, and an unrelated modified tracked file. Then:

  • The verb lands a merge whose parents are the base and the head. The working tree and the origin's main match it.
  • A conflicting head is refused, and afterwards git status --porcelain output and the index are byte-identical to before. No MERGE_HEAD exists.
  • With index.lock held through the first attempt, the verb retries and lands.
  • --candidate lands that exact commit.
  • A message with no body is refused.

Negative control: with the conflict refusal removed, the conflict case leaves conflict state in the checkout, and the byte-identity assertion fails. The log goes under the run directory. tests/test_crew_help_names_its_state.py and tests/test_skill_contracts.py pass with the new verb. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. The coordinator runs the importer population at merge. Write paths: a new module reckon/crew/merge.py, reckon/crew_run_commands.py, skills/reckon-build/SKILL.md and the test file.

§43 — A landed merge whose push fails says it landed

Why. On 2026-10-17, reckon-8a landed a reviewed head with reckon crew merge (§42) from the fleet allocation. The merge was built and fast-forwarded as ba6e5fc19. The push then failed with "ssh: connect to host github.com port 22: Connection refused", because compute nodes refuse GitHub SSH on port 22. The verb exited 1 with an error and did not say the merge had landed. A coordinator retrying it could rebuild or double-handle a merge already in place.

Deliverable. When the push fails after the fast-forward has landed, the verb reports the landed merge commit and the base. It states that only the push is outstanding, and exits with a status distinct from a refusal that wrote nothing. A rerun for the same head finds the merge already in HEAD and goes straight to the push, rather than building a second merge. On a host where port 22 to GitHub is refused, the push uses the port-443 route, either the caller's GIT_SSH_COMMAND or a stated fallback. The reckon-build skill's integrate step says so.

Done when tests/test_a_reviewed_head_lands_without_touching_the_index.py gains three cases. A push that fails after the fast-forward yields a JSON document naming the landed commit, with a distinct exit status. A rerun for the same head pushes without building a new merge. A third case runs with GIT_SSH_COMMAND unset and an ssh stub on PATH that refuses port 22. It finds the push retried by the stated port-443 route and the merge reported pushed. A row in tests/test_skill_contracts.py finds that the reckon-build skill's integrate step names that route. Negative control: against the base tree, the failed push reports a bare error with no landed commit. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Write paths: reckon/crew/merge.py, reckon/crew_run_commands.py, skills/reckon-build/SKILL.md, tests/test_skill_contracts.py and that test file. It extends what §42 built, which has landed.

§44 — A reload tree in use on any host is not pruned

Why. At about 17:08Z on 2026-10-17, reckon-cd's follower logged "deferred its reload: could not materialize HEAD ([Errno 2] No such file or directory: ~/.config/reckon/crew/reload-trees/4695b8e9e67d6131/752681b41…)". That commit was main's HEAD at 17:07:54Z. By 17:10Z the directory held only two newer trees, and later reloads went through. materialize_committed_tree in reckon/checkout_trust.py publishes a tree by rename, or finds one already complete. Only then does pin_materialized_tree open .in-use and take a shared flock. Between those steps, a concurrent materialiser sorts the images by directory mtime, keeps COMMITTED_TREE_RETENTION (3), and removes any older one whose exclusive flock it can take. A tree published but not yet pinned is removable, which matches the error.

Corrected after the plan review of 2026-10-17. The retention prune does not explain this incident. It removes only trees ranked past the newest three, and any prune leaves at least three standing. At 17:08Z, 752681b41 was main's newest commit, so its tree ranked first. By 17:10Z the directory held two trees, not three. Something other than the retention prune removed trees, and §46 names it. This section still closes the publish-to-lease gap and the cross-host lease gap, because the code admits both whatever removed 752681b41.

Re-corrected after §46's investigation. The paragraph above was wrong. The imas-codex session's follower logged "removed old reload tree …/752681b41…" at 17:10:12.711Z. That was the retention prune at shutil.rmtree(entry), and reckon-cd's follower deferred 2 ms later, the losing side of the same race. The prune ranks trees by directory mtime, not commit order. Every follower touches the tree it loads, and four commits landed in 137 s, so main's newest commit could rank past the newest three. Several pruners running at once can also leave fewer than three trees between them. This section's original account stands, and the plan review's objection rested on reading the rank as commit order. §46 also found a race this section does not close: a pruner whose entry a peer removed first raises out of the prune loop and aborts its reload. §47 carries that.

A second gap sits behind it. flock does not cross nodes on this GPFS home, measured on 2026-10-15 for the producer seat. So a lease held by a follower on another host does not stop a pruner here from removing the tree that follower imports from. Each follower renews its lease's mtime on every poll (refresh_materialized_tree_pin), but pruning never reads that mtime. The loss surfaces later, as a failed lazy import in the host whose tree vanished.

Deliverable. A materialiser holds the new tree's lease before the tree becomes visible to pruning, or pruning skips a complete tree whose .complete marker is younger than a stated grace. Pruning also skips any tree whose .in-use mtime was renewed within a stated window, whatever flock says, so a lease renewed from another host keeps its tree. A materialiser that still meets a vanished target retries once before deferring. Do not change the retention count or the producer's reload path otherwise.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_reload_tree_in_use_on_any_host_is_not_pruned.py, covers three cases. A tree published by one materialiser and pruned by a second, with the first not yet pinned, is still present when the first pins it. A tree whose .in-use mtime was renewed inside the window, with no flock held (standing in for another host), survives a prune that would otherwise take it. A tree past both windows with no lease is pruned as before. A fourth case removes the target between publication and pinning on the first attempt. It finds the materialiser retries once and returns a pinned tree, and that a second removal makes it defer with the vanished path named. Negative control: against the base tree, the first two cases lose their tree, and the fourth defers on the first removal. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Write paths: reckon/checkout_trust.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and the test file.

§45 — Reload-tree housekeeping stays off the monitor stream

Why. Since §41 landed on 2026-10-17, every follower reloads onto the committed head whenever main's HEAD moves. Every promotion moves it, so that is every few minutes. After materialize_committed_tree prunes, crew_follow_commands.py (near line 420) writes "reckon … removed old reload tree <path>" for each pruned tree. It also writes "reckon … kept reload tree <path>: live image lease" for each tree whose lease held. Both go to self.stream, the ticker stream that each session's crew-host Monitor relays as events. The lead reported the noise from a live monitor that evening: two removals and four keeps among three run transitions, with one tree reported kept twice in a row. A kept tree is re-reported on every reload for as long as its lease lives. By then the reckon producer's service log held 24 removal lines and imas-codex's 12 keep lines, and every session's own follower prints the same again. Neither line asks the reader to do anything.

Deliverable. The reload path writes no line to the ticker stream for a tree it removed or kept as expected. It records the last prune on the seat's watch record, beside reload_deferral: when it ran, how many trees it removed, and which trees a lease kept. A diagnosis can then read it without a stream. One line still reaches the stream when the prune itself fails, which is a removal that raised. Do not change what is pruned; that is §44's.

Done when a new test, tests/test_reload_tree_housekeeping_stays_off_the_stream.py, drives a follower reload that prunes two trees and keeps one under a live lease. It finds no line on the ticker stream naming a reload tree, and the seat's watch record carries the removed count and the kept path. A second case makes one removal raise and finds exactly one line naming that failure. Negative control: against the base tree, the first case's stream carries the removed and kept lines. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Build it after §44, which also writes reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and the test file.

§46 — Name what removed the newest reload tree

Why. At about 17:08Z on 2026-10-17, reckon-cd's follower deferred a reload: "could not materialize HEAD ([Errno 2] No such file or directory: …/reload-trees/4695b8e9e67d6131/752681b41…)". 752681b41 was main's newest commit, and two minutes later only two trees stood. The retention prune in materialize_committed_tree keeps the newest three. So neither the newest tree's removal nor a two-tree directory is its doing, as the plan review of §44 showed. The removal path is unknown. Candidates include two followers pruning the same entry at once, a failed rename leaving a partial tree, an os.utime on a target another process replaced, a sweep of the crew home, or a host-local /tmp or cache cleaner. Until it is named, §44's fix may harden windows this incident never used.

Deliverable. An investigation, with no product change. It reads the follower and producer service logs under ~/.config/reckon/logs/ around 17:05–17:12Z, and the streams of runs live then, for any removal under reload-trees/. It reproduces the error text from the code: it finds every call in materialize_committed_tree and its prune whose FileNotFoundError names the tree directory itself rather than a file inside it. It also lists every code path in reckon that can remove a directory under reload-trees/.

Done when the manifest names the removal path, with the log line or code line that shows it. The scan's positive control: the same log scan finds a removal known to have happened. For example, a "removed old reload tree" line from a follower log after 15:52Z must surface, so an absence in the 17:05–17:12Z window means the record holds none. A candidate list may close the section only when every removal path in reckon's code has been listed and the logs searched for each, and the manifest says which evidence is missing that would settle it. It also states whether the change built by the reload-tree pruning section (§44) closes that path. Nothing under reckon/ or tests/ changes. Evidence goes under the run directory. Write path: the run's report directory.

§47 — A reload never aborts on a tree a peer pruned first

Why. §46 traced reckon-cd's 17:10Z deferral to two followers pruning the same reload-tree directory at once. The imas-codex follower removed 752681b41 at 17:10:12.711Z, and reckon-cd's follower raised FileNotFoundError naming that directory 2 ms later. Three calls in materialize_committed_tree (reckon/checkout_trust.py) name the directory itself when it vanishes: os.utime(target), the entry.stat() sort key while ranking images, and shutil.rmtree(entry). The loser's exception leaves the prune and aborts its reload. §44's retry covers only a vanished target, not a vanished entry ranked for removal. The prune also ranks by directory mtime. Only materialize_committed_tree writes that mtime, through os.utime(target), so every follower that materialises a head touches that head's tree. During HEAD churn (four commits in 137 s that evening) that ranking stops tracking recency, and a tree published moments ago ranks old.

Deliverable. Ranking and pruning tolerate an entry that vanishes underneath them. A tree a peer already removed counts as removed. It is never an error, and it never aborts the reload of the tree being materialised. The publish grace from §44 covers at least a measured HEAD-churn window. The first step states the window it uses and why. Do not change the retention count.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_reload_never_aborts_on_a_tree_a_peer_pruned.py, covers four cases. In the first, two materialisers prune the same old entry, one removing it during the other's ranking or removal, and both return their own pinned tree. In the second, an entry vanishes between the directory listing and the stat() sort key, and the ranking skips it. In the third, a tree published 137 s ago, the churn window measured that evening, ranks behind three trees touched more recently, and a prune keeps it. The same tree published past the stated grace is pruned as before. In the fourth, a tree is removed while the materialiser walks it, raising ENOTDIR as seen on e4acca00, or while it pins it, raising ENOENT on .in-use as seen on 3cffef06. Each time, the reload retries or skips and returns a pinned tree. Negative control: against the base tree, the first and fourth cases raise FileNotFoundError or NotADirectoryError out of materialize_committed_tree, and the third case prunes the tree published 137 s ago. Each red case is named in the control log. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Build it after §44, which also writes reckon/checkout_trust.py. Write paths: reckon/checkout_trust.py and the test file.

§48 — A stale lease record does not expose a tree being pinned

Why. §44's last review (head 169b93999) found one remaining window. It was folded into §47's node, and §47 did not address it. _prune_must_keep in reckon/checkout_trust.py keeps a leased tree only when its lease record names another node renewed within COMMITTED_TREE_LEASE_WINDOW_SECONDS. Suppose the record still names a host that stopped renewing longer ago than that, and a third host is between taking its flock in pin_materialized_tree and rewriting the record. A pruner on another node then sees only the stale name, takes its node-local exclusive lock, and removes the tree being pinned. The window is a couple of syscalls wide.

Deliverable. A tree whose lease record is stale but whose .in-use was opened or renewed within the publish grace counts as in use. Alternatively, the pin rewrites its node record before it takes the flock, so the record never names only a stale host while a pin is in progress. The first step states which, and why. Do not change the retention count, the grace or the lease window.

Done when a new test, tests/test_a_stale_lease_record_does_not_expose_a_pin.py, seeds a tree whose lease record names a host last renewed past the lease window. The tree's existing .in-use carries that stale host's old mtime, also past the window, because a pin's os.open(…, O_CREAT) of an existing file does not refresh it. The test must not create a fresh .in-use. It pauses a pin between its two steps, runs a prune from a second materialiser, and finds the tree kept and the pin completing. A tree with a stale record and no pin in progress is pruned as before. Negative control: against the base tree, the paused pin loses its tree. The log goes under the run directory. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Build it after §47, which also writes reckon/checkout_trust.py. Write paths: reckon/checkout_trust.py and the test file.

§49 — A watcher armed from a session carries none of that session's identity

Why. On 2026-10-18 at 03:32Z this session's pane (f653840f) drew a burst of rows dated five days earlier. They included codex-sub lift grants and ends at 08:54–09:50 (+02:00) on 2026-10-13, and reviews of the-order-test-fails-when-a-queued-run-holds-a-slot being dropped and queued. All of them belonged to session fleet-move-20261007, which had been idle since 2026-10-07.

The §33 dispatch of a-read-answers-inside-its-deadline re-armed the reckon watcher. It went through _ensure_watch_producer and then _start_watch_producer in reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py, on the process route: supervisor pid 861123, parent pid 1. That subprocess.Popen passes no env=, so the producer inherited the dispatching Claude session's environment: CLAUDE_PID=2226045, CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID=f653840f…, CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_SOCKET and CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN.

At 03:31:19Z the watcher's sweep of sessions nobody follows (§37, runs.sweep_unfollowed_sessions) composed a review of that session's run r-20261008T104759434567. The dispatch resolved its coordinator from the inherited variables, so the review's coordinator.runtime_session_id reads f653840f. It resolved the session host from CLAUDE_PID (_session_host_owner), so it handed a follower for fleet-move-20261007 to this session's host, pid 2226630. That follower's replay is §50.

The systemd unit route already builds a clean environment in _watcher_service_environment, carrying only PATH and RECKON_HOME. The process route and the fleet spawn route both run the supervisor argv with whatever environment their spawner had.

Deliverable. The producer runs with no coordinator session's identity, whichever route started it:

  • One list in reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py names the Claude Code session variables. It covers every variable that dispatch_accounting._coordinator_runtime and _session_host_owner consult, and at least CLAUDE_PID, every CLAUDE_CODE_* variable, CLAUDECODE, CODEX_SESSION_ID and CODEX_THREAD_ID, so a producer armed from a Codex session sheds its identity too.
  • _start_watch_producer passes an environment without them.
  • The supervisor script _WATCH_PRODUCER_SUPERVISOR removes them before it starts the producer, so the fleet route is covered too.
  • A producer reload keeps its pid and its environment. So crew_watch in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py also removes them from os.environ before its first sweep. A producer already running with a session's identity then sheds it at its next reload.

Done when tests/test_a_watcher_carries_no_session_identity.py shows all four of these:

  • (a) With CLAUDE_PID, CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID, CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_SOCKET, CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN, CODEX_SESSION_ID and CODEX_THREAD_ID set in the caller, the environment _start_watch_producer hands to its launcher carries none of them, and still carries PATH and RECKON_HOME. The test also sets each of those six variables alone and finds that _coordinator_runtime() and _session_host_owner(), run in the scrubbed environment, name no session. If one of them consults a variable the list does not cover, this check fails.
  • (b) crew_watch, entered with them set, finds _session_host_owner() returning None and _coordinator_runtime() naming no Claude session before its first sweep.
  • (c) A review dispatched from inside such a producer records no coordinator runtime session and writes no session-host request.
  • (d) The supervisor script _WATCH_PRODUCER_SUPERVISOR, run directly with the six variables in its own environment and a stub producer argv that writes its environment to a file, starts a producer whose environment carries none of them. The fleet route's batch step runs this same supervisor with the batch step's environment, so this case covers the fleet route.

The negative control passes the inherited environment through, and (a) must then fail. It is logged under the run directory. The test files importing reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. This section runs before §50, because both write reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. Write paths: reckon/crew/dispatch_watch.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_watcher_carries_no_session_identity.py.

§50 — A follower re-armed on a stale checkpoint does not replay the days since

Why. The follower §49 handed to this session's host was for fleet-move-20261007. That session's durable place, the checkpoint reckon/crew/follow_checkpoint.py writes into the follower directory, had last been written days earlier. _follow_resume_plan in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py returned continue at the recorded offset, because follow_checkpoint.continues checks only the stream's identity, not the record's age.

The follower then delivered every matching row written since: lift grants and ends from 2026-10-13 06:54Z onward, and that session's queue transitions from 2026-10-14. Each was drawn under its original time, and there were enough of them that the Monitor suppressed several batches for exceeding its output rate.

The margin replay exists so that a re-arm minutes after a detach does not skip what happened meanwhile, as follow_checkpoint's module docstring describes. The pane's history replay beside it is capped at DEFAULT_HISTORY_SECONDS (four hours); the margin replay has no cap.

Deliverable. A re-arm whose checkpoint is older than the pane's history window resumes in _follow_resume_plan's baseline mode instead of replaying the margin: it reports each run's current state from live run state rather than reading the rows written since the checkpoint. A run that has not moved is not announced again (§25). The suppression goes through the same delivered-state record a fresh arm already reads, runs.read_delivered (the follower's .delivered.json), compared by _follow_gap_rows. It does not use the checkpoint's reported map, which only the in-place reload path consumes. A margin within the window is delivered as before. The window is the pane's history window as _follow_history_caps in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py resolves it: the project's flight config ticker.history_window, falling back to DEFAULT_HISTORY_SECONDS. The staleness check calls that reader rather than reading the constant, so the margin and the history replay cannot disagree. The record's age comes from a stamp the record carries. If no stamp exists yet, the worker adds one, and a record written before the stamp is aged by its file's modification time.

Done when tests/test_a_stale_checkpoint_replays_no_old_margin.py shows all four of these:

  • (a) A checkpoint written five days ago at offset X, with rows observed five days ago appended after X, draws none of those rows on re-arm. A run whose recorded state still matches draws no row.
  • (b) A checkpoint written ten minutes ago, with rows after X, draws each of them once, as it does today.
  • (c) A run whose state changed during a stale margin draws one row for its current state.
  • (d) With ticker.history_window set to one hour, a checkpoint written two hours ago resumes as a baseline. With it set to one day, the same checkpoint delivers its margin.

The negative control removes the age check, and (a) must then fail. It is logged under the run directory. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py or reckon/crew/follow_checkpoint.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. This section runs after §49, because both write reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. Write paths: reckon/crew/follow_checkpoint.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_stale_checkpoint_replays_no_old_margin.py.

§51 — A pane draws a lift once, however many sessions its host follows

Why. At 06:55:13Z on 2026-10-18 this session's pane drew each codex-sub lift transition twice in the same second: once with this session's counters (3w) and once with another's (0w … 1d). The pane's session host (pid 2226630) runs two followers. One serves this session. The other serves fleet-move-20261007, handed to the host by the watcher defect §49 fixed. Each follower draws the lift, because §23's once-per-pane claim is keyed on the session: lift_claim_dir(session) in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py makes one claim directory per session, not per pane. A pane is one session host, which can follow more than one crew session, for instance a coordinator dispatching under two session names. A follower handed over by mistake stays until the owning Claude process ends, because the host restarts a signalled child and has no release request. §49 stops such mistakes at their source, so this section does not add a release path; it makes a pane draw a lift once whichever sessions its host follows.

Deliverable. Every follower a session host runs shares one lift claim per pane, keyed on the host that spawned them, the RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER pair SessionHost._spawn stamps on each child and follower_owner() in reckon/crew/follower_registration.py reads back. One host serves one pane, so a host key is a pane key. A follower is hosted exactly when RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER is present in its environment. follower_owner() itself falls back to the parent pid when the variable is absent, so the presence check is the signal that selects the host key or the per-session key, not the reader's return value. A follower with no host keeps the per-session key. A lift transition then draws one row per pane, whichever follower reaches it first.

Done when a test with one host running followers for two sessions, and one lift granted and then ended, finds one grant row and one end row in the host's output, not two of each. A follower without a host still draws the lift once for its session. The negative control keys the claim on the session again, and the two-session case then draws two rows. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew/session_host.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. This section runs after §50, because both write reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. A follower that has not reloaded onto the change keeps the per-session key, so while an old and a new follower share a pane each may draw the same lift once. That transient ends when both have reloaded, and this section does not guard it. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_pane_draws_a_lift_once_per_host.py.

§52 — A pane's lift-claim key names a real host and its node

Why. §51 (run r-20261018T075720374567, merged as 7ab6d95a5) keys a pane's lift claim on RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER when that variable is present, as host-<pid>-<start> under crew_home/watch/lifts. Its plan review found two defects in that key:

  • Presence is the wrong signal. The follower's own reloader writes RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER into its replacement image unconditionally (reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, the re-exec near line 573, from follower_owner(), which always returns a pair). So an unhosted follower presents the variable after its first reload and takes a host key it has no host for.
  • The key names no node. A pid and start tick identify a process only on one node. A sweep reading host-<pid>-<start> cannot tell a host that died here from one alive on another node that shares the home.

Deliverable. lift_claim_key decides that a follower is hosted from RECKON_SESSION_HOST_SETTLE. Only SessionHost._spawn in reckon/crew/session_host.py sets that variable, and a reload's exec carries it through unchanged. A hosted follower's key names its node as well as the host pair, as host-<node>-<pid>-<start>. An unhosted follower keeps the per-session key, before and after any number of reloads.

Done when a test shows all three of these:

  • (a) An unhosted follower that has reloaded, with RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER in its environment and no RECKON_SESSION_HOST_SETTLE, takes the per-session key.
  • (b) A hosted follower's key carries the node name.
  • (c) §51's two-session case still draws one grant row and one end row.

The negative control decides hosted by RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER's presence again, and (a) must then fail. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py or reckon/crew/session_host.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. §51's test fixture in tests/test_a_pane_draws_a_lift_once_per_host.py simulates a hosted follower by setting only RECKON_FOLLOWER_OWNER. It must also set RECKON_SESSION_HOST_SETTLE, as a real host does, or its two hosted cases fail under the corrected signal. Build it after §51 merges. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, tests/test_a_lift_claim_key_names_a_real_host.py and tests/test_a_pane_draws_a_lift_once_per_host.py.

§53 — Lift-claim directories of hosts that ended on this node are swept

Why. With §51 and §52, every session-host lifetime creates its own lift-claim directory, host-<node>-<pid>-<start> under crew_home/watch/lifts. Nothing removes the directories of hosts that have ended: _prune_lift_claims in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py removes only claim files inside the current pane's directory, and runs only when a lift ends. §51's review also found a stale comment in reckon/crew/ticker.py near line 1226 that still describes the claim as per-session.

Deliverable. A follower's prune pass also removes host-keyed claim directories whose node is this node and whose host no longer runs. Liveness is decided by pid together with start tick, as owner_alive in reckon/crew/session_host.py decides it, so a reused pid does not keep a dead host's directory. A directory naming another node is never removed. A directory left in §51's node-less format is removed only after it has gone untouched for seven days. Per-session directories are left alone. The ticker.py comment states the host keying.

Done when a test with five directories finds, after one prune pass, two removed and three kept. The removed two are a dead local host's directory and a node-less directory untouched for more than seven days. The kept three are a live local host's directory, a directory for a host on another node whose pid is absent here, and a node-less directory touched within seven days. The negative control drops the node check, and the other node's directory is then removed. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py or reckon/crew/ticker.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Build it after §52 merges. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py, reckon/crew/ticker.py and tests/test_abandoned_lift_claim_directories_are_swept.py.

§54 — A commit that changes no reckon source moves no follower or producer

Why. On 2026-10-18 the lead's pane printed this pair twice within twelve minutes: "producer nova runs older code than this follower (2bc6f84f vs d957dd68); it is reloading itself, waiting for it to catch up" and "…its seat runs on 98dci4-srv-1006.iter.org and reloads itself when the code changes, so it will catch up on its own". The second pair was the same with da08bcc9. 2bc6f84f is a commit that edits only a plan document.

Since §41, a reckon process's code identity is the commit at the checkout's HEAD: checkout_trust.source_code_identity returns committed_head. Every commit to main therefore moves every follower's and producer's identity, whether or not it changes code: plan edits, run-state writes, promotion and release records. Between 13:00Z and 15:00Z that day main took 141 commits, of which 2 touched reckon/. So each follower and producer reloaded about seventy times more often than its code changed, and a new reload tree was extracted for each commit. A producer on another node is nearly always a commit or two behind, so once each follower's reload window closes, _producer_code_events in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py prints the pair. That is two lines per foreign producer per commit, and the second line repeats what the first says.

Deliverable. A reckon process's code identity names the content of the reckon/ package at HEAD, for example the tree object HEAD:reckon, not the commit. A commit that changes no file under reckon/ moves no identity, so it reloads no follower or producer and extracts no reload tree. Reload trees are keyed and extracted by that identity. A running image still reports the identity it loaded, so a follower and a producer on the same code compare equal however many commits sit between them.

Three sites read or use the identity, and all three change together:

  • checkout_trust.source_code_identity returns the reckon/ tree id, from git rev-parse HEAD:reckon.
  • _FollowerReloader.poll in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py reads that same identity in place of committed_head.
  • materialize_committed_tree extracts with git archive --format=tar --prefix=reckon/ <tree>, because a tree object holds the package's contents without the reckon/ directory. The image directory keeps the reckon package at its root and is named by the tree id, which running_code_identity already reads as 40 hexadecimal digits.

The pane line a foreign producer's overrun prints is §55.

Done when a test with a temporary repository finds:

  • (a) A commit that touches only a file outside reckon/ leaves the source identity unchanged, and a follower holding the previous identity reports no stale image and starts no reload.
  • (b) A commit that changes a file under reckon/ moves the identity, and the reload materializes a tree for it.
  • (c) A follower and a producer whose images came from different commits with the same reckon/ content compare equal.

The negative control keys the identity on the commit again, and case (a) then reloads. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/checkout_trust.py, reckon/crew/follower_registration.py or reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Followers and producers already running keep commit identities until they reload once onto the change, so the change must accept a commit identity from an old image. Write paths: reckon/checkout_trust.py, reckon/crew/follower_registration.py, reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_commit_without_code_moves_no_identity.py.

§55 — A foreign producer's overrun is one line on the pane

Why. When a producer whose seat is on another node stays behind a follower past the reload window, _producer_code_events in reckon/crew_follow_commands.py puts two lines on the pane together. The first is "…runs older code than this follower (…); it is reloading itself, waiting for it to catch up". The second is "…its seat runs on <host> and reloads itself when the code changes, so it will catch up on its own". The second line says what the first already said, plus the host. The lead's pane on 2026-10-18 printed the pair twice in twelve minutes for the nova producer seated on 98dci4-srv-1006. §54 removes most occurrences by not reloading on commits that change no code, but a genuine code change still produces the pair.

Deliverable. For a producer whose seat is on another node, an overrun puts one line on the pane. That line names the producer, both identities and the seat's host, and says the producer reloads itself. For a producer on this host the remedy line is unchanged: it names the cycle command a reader can run. The JSON stream keeps both events in every case.

Done when a test with a foreign seat that overruns the reload window finds one pane line carrying the host. It finds both events in the JSON stream. With a seat on this host, it finds the reloading line and the cycle-command line as today. The negative control restores the two-line pane output for a foreign seat, and the one-line case then fails. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Build it after §54, because both write reckon/crew_follow_commands.py. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_foreign_producer_overrun_is_one_line.py.

§56 — A pane counts its session's workers across every project it follows

Why. On 2026-10-18 the lead reported that a pane's worker count was "all over the place". Within 17 minutes, 20:17 to 20:34 (+02:00), session 83d14db1's pane printed 7w, 8w, 7w, 3w, 7w, 2w, 7w, 0w and 8w. Each figure was right for its own project. The session works in two projects, and its own working count was 6 to 8 in power_convertor and 0 to 2 in reckon at the same moments. At 18:34:12Z the reckon row reserved-lane-nested-reask carries the session's reckon count, 0. At 18:34:49Z the power_convertor row switching-event-localisation carries its power_convertor count, 8. The evidence is in reckon-18dc8a86709b.events and power_convertor-2f320d946ced.events under the crew watch directory. The session host runs one follower per project and session into one pane. _scoped_watch_event in reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py takes a row's counters from that row's own project's session_counts entry. So consecutive rows report different populations under one unlabelled column, and the figure jumps whenever the rows alternate between projects.

Deliverable. For a follower scoped to a session, a row's counters describe that session's work across every project. _scoped_watch_event takes the row's own project's figures from the row's event, as it does now. It adds the session's entry from the newest event that carries session_counts in each other project's events file under the watch directory. A project whose newest such event has no entry for the session adds nothing. Each other file is read from its tail, never in full, so the cost of a row does not grow with an events file's history. A follower with no session is one project's pane and keeps that project's counts. The JSON stream keeps the per-project figures it carries today and adds the session-wide ones beside them, so a consumer of either reading loses nothing.

Done when a test in a temporary crew home with two projects' events files has session S at 7 working in project A and 1 in project B. A row from B renders 8w, and so does a row from A. After a B transition that takes S's B count to 0, the next A row renders 7w. A project whose newest event has no entry for S adds nothing. A follower with no session renders its project's counts as today. The JSON event carries both the project's figures and the session-wide ones. The negative control restores the row-project-only counters, and the B row then renders 1w. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. Do not build it while read-deadline §40 or §41 is in flight, because they also write reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py. Write paths: reckon/crew/recovery_watch.py and tests/test_a_pane_counts_its_session_across_projects.py.

§60 — A reloaded process finds the fleet script its dispatch needs

Why. Since 21:22:49Z on 2026-10-18, every automatic review dispatch has failed with the reckon installation is missing its worktree fleet script; searched: …/crew/reload-trees/4695b8e9e67d6131/855503e2…/skills, …/reckon/_skills. That is nine runs across reckon and other projects by 22:15Z, recorded in each live pointer's review_dispatch.reason. The last automatic dispatch recorded as succeeding was at 03:33Z. So every review has had to be dispatched by hand, and a session that does not watch its obligations gets none. _fleet_script in reckon/crew/routing.py resolves skills/reckon-build/scripts/worktree_fleet.py beside the running package, or under reckon/_skills. A process reloaded into a tree that materialize_committed_tree extracted has only reckon/ there, so the lookup fails. Reload trees also held only reckon/ before §54, yet dispatch worked then. And §54 merged at 19:10Z, two hours before the first failure. So the trigger is not yet known. Candidates are §54's tree-keyed reload, which makes processes stay in reload trees across commits that change no code, and the dispatch-path commits merged between 21:01Z and 21:10Z: 054c27156, fff27c183 and da84fdfdc.

Deliverable. First, name the change that made dispatch from a reloaded process start failing, with evidence logged under the run directory, and rule out the other candidates. Then make a reloaded process find the fleet script at its own version. The reload tree carries the skills/ directory from the same revision as its reckon/ package, and the code identity covers both trees, so a change to the fleet script reloads processes and a change outside both moves nothing. An image that already exists, holding only reckon/, reloads once into a tree that has both.

Done when the report names the commit that introduced a reload image holding reckon/ alone. §61 — A reloaded process launches through its own interpreter

Why. Once §60 landed, the review reflex dispatched a review automatically at 23:58:11Z, the first since 21:22Z. Its launch then failed at once, with ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'pydantic' (/home/ITER/mcintos/.config/reckon/crew/runs/r-20261018T235712431158-review-of-reload-carries-skills-population-at-merge-b6558f0d1cab/stderr.log). The launch argv starts with the reload tree's reckon/host_shims/srun. That shim takes repo_root as two directories above itself, which in a reload tree is the tree root. It then picks the first interpreter among $RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON, $repo_root/.venv/bin/python, python3.12 on PATH and python3. A reload tree has no .venv, so it falls through to a system python3.12 that cannot import the package's dependencies. No automatic dispatch had reached this step before, because each one failed earlier on the missing fleet script. So every launch a reloaded producer or follower makes through a shim fails the same way.

Deliverable. A process running from a reload image launches its shims with the interpreter it runs under. The reloader exports RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON as its own sys.executable when it re-executes into an image, unless a value is already set, so every shim the reloaded process invokes resolves that interpreter first. A process outside a reload image is unchanged.

Done when a test re-executes a follower into a materialised image and finds RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON in the exec environment set to the reloading process's interpreter, while a value already set is kept. A second test runs the image's srun shim with a PATH whose python3.12 is a stub that exits non-zero with a marker. The shim resolves the exported interpreter and never runs the stub. The negative control drops the export, and the second test then reaches the stub. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew_follow_commands.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. After merge, the coordinator confirms that an automatic review both dispatches and launches. Write paths: reckon/crew_follow_commands.py and tests/test_a_reloaded_process_launches_through_its_interpreter.py.

§62 — A launch names the interpreter of the process that composed it

Why. §61 merged at 01:06:19Z. Twenty-seven seconds later the review reflex dispatched a review, and its launch failed with the same ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'pydantic', through the reload tree 4c356bfc's srun shim. By 01:12Z every follower and producer had reloaded in place into tree 6288962b. Their /proc/<pid>/environ shows that none of the 25 carries RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON. §61 exports the variable when the reloader re-executes, but that reload was performed by the code each process was already running, which predates §61. So the export reaches a process only on its next reload, which waits for the next change to reckon/ or skills/. Until then automatic launches keep failing. Any fix that depends on how a process was last reloaded has the same gap.

Deliverable. The environment a launch is given carries RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON set to the composing process's own sys.executable, unless one is already set. It is set where reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py composes the persisted launch environment, beside the PATH that launch_search_path builds. So a shim on that PATH resolves the interpreter of the process that composed the launch, however that process was started or reloaded.

Done when a test composes a launch environment and finds RECKON_SHIM_PYTHON equal to sys.executable, with a value already set in the caller's environment kept. A second test runs a shim from a directory with no .venv, under the composed environment and a PATH whose python3.12 is a stub that exits non-zero with a marker. The shim reaches the composing interpreter and never the stub. The negative control removes the setting, and the second test then reaches the stub. It is logged under the run directory, and every log ends in EXIT=. The test files importing reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py show no added failure against base. ruff check and ruff format --check add no findings on the changed Python files. After merge, the coordinator confirms that an automatic review launches without the workaround prefix. Write paths: reckon/crew/dispatch_launch.py and tests/test_a_launch_names_its_composers_interpreter.py.

§63 — A reload image carries the model catalogue

The reload tree 4695b8e9e67d6131/fa38bebd… has no docs/. This is the same shape as §60, where the image lacked the fleet script.

Deliverable. A reload image carries docs/state/reckon/model-catalogue.yaml from the same revision as its reckon/ and skills/ trees, at the same relative path, so model_catalogue_path resolves it unchanged. The code identity covers the catalogue as well: a catalogue change reloads processes so they read the new models, and a change outside the three moves nothing.
